\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,t]}\left(\phi\left(X_{s-}+h(\eta-\delta_{(y,s)},y,s)\right)-\phi\left(X_{s-}\right)\right)\eta(dy,ds)\qquad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

(B.22)

Now, similarly,

\int_{U_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\phi^{\prime}\left(X^{(m)}_{s}\right)h(\eta,x,s)\right|\lambda(dx)ds\\
\leqslant\left(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{N}\\
s\in[0,1]\end{subarray}}\left|\phi^{\prime}\left(X^{(m)}_{s}\right)\right|\right)\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{t}\left|h(\eta,x,s)\right|\lambda(dx)ds<\infty\qquad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

(B.23)

and by dominated convergence

\int_{U_{m}}\int_{0}^{t}\phi^{\prime}\left(X^{(m)}_{s}\right)h(\eta,x,s)\lambda(dx)ds\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{t}\phi^{\prime}\left(X_{s}\right)h(\eta,x,s)\lambda(dx)ds\qquad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

(B.24)

The fact that
\phi\left(X^{(m)}_{t}\right)\rightarrow\phi\left(X_{t}\right)
a.s. follows by continuity of
\phi
. This concludes the proof. ∎

Appendix C
Proofs of Theorem
4.2
and Corollaries
4.3
and
4.7

Lemma C.1
.

For
p\in(1,2]
, the function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:x\mapsto|x|^{p}
is continuously differentiable and its derivative
\phi^{\prime}
is
(p-1)
-Hölder continuous with Hölder constant
c_{\phi}=p2^{2-p}
.

Proof.

We want to show that

c_{\phi}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\neq b\\
a,b\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{|\phi^{\prime}(a)-\phi^{\prime}(b)|}{|a-b|^{p-1}}=p2^{2-p}.

(C.1)

First we observe that
\phi^{\prime}(x)=p\sgn(x)|x|^{p-1}
, with the convention that
sgn(0)=1
. Let
a\neq b
and assume without loss of generality that
|a|\geqslant|b|\geqslant 0
. Then
a\neq 0
and

\frac{\left|\sgn(a)|a|^{p-1}-\sgn(b)|b|^{p-1}\right|}{|a-b|^{p-1}}=\frac{\left|1-\sgn\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\left|\frac{b}{a}\right|^{p-1}\right|}{\left|1-\frac{b}{a}\right|^{p-1}}.

(C.2)

It follows that

c_{\phi}=p\sup_{x\in[-1,1)}\frac{1-\sgn(x)\left|x\right|^{p-1}}{(1-x)^{p-1}}=:p\sup_{x\in[-1,1)}f(x)

(C.3)

The function
f
is differentiable on
[-1,1)
with derivative
f^{\prime}(x)=(p-1)(1-x)^{-p}(1-|x|^{p-2})\leqslant 0
, so
f
is decreasing and therefore

f(x)\leqslant f(-1)=2^{2-p}.

(C.4)

We conclude that
c_{\phi}=p2^{2-p}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

We start by showing the result for an approximation of
h
. Let
(U_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{X}
s.t.
\bigcup_{m}U_{m}=\mathbb{X}
and
\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda(U_{m})<\infty
and
U_{m}\subset U_{m+1}
. Define for
\mu\in\mathbf{N}
,
(y,s)\in\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]
:

h_{m}(\mu,y,s):=\left[(h(\mu,y,s)\wedge m)\vee(-m)\right]\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\in U_{m}\}}.

(C.5)

Now
h_{m}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])\cap L^{2}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
and
h_{m}\rightarrow h
in
L^{2}
as
m\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover,
|h_{m}|\leqslant|h|
and
|\MalD_{(x,t)}h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|\leqslant|\MalD_{(x,t)}h(\eta,y,s)|
for all
(x,t),(y,s)\in\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]
. This implies that
h_{m}
satisfies (
2.12
) and hence
h_{m}\in\dom\delta
.

Finally,
h_{m}
is also bounded. In particular, for any
\mu\in\mathbf{N}
,
(y,s)\in\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]
,

|h_{m}(\mu,y,s)|\leqslant m\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\in U_{m}\}}.

(C.6)

We conclude that
\delta(h_{m})
is well-defined and has by (
2.13
) the pathwise expression

\delta(h_{m})=\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]}h_{m}(\eta-\delta_{(y,s)},y,s)\eta(dy,ds)-\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}h_{m}(\eta,y,s)\lambda(dy)ds.

(C.7)

Define
X_{t}
as in Theorem
4.1
with
h=h_{m}
and
X_{0}=0
. Then
\delta(h_{m})=X_{1}
and we infer from (
4.2
) that

\phi(\delta(h_{m}))-\phi(0)=\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]}\left(\phi(X_{s-}+h_{m}(\eta-\delta_{(y,s)},y,s))-\phi(X_{s-})\right)\eta(dy,ds)\\
-\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})h_{m}(\eta,y,s)\lambda(dy)ds\qquad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

(C.8)

We would now like to take expectations on both sides of (
C.8
), but in order to do so, we must first show that the terms in question are in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
. Start with a simple estimate for
\phi
that uses Hölder continuity of
\phi^{\prime}
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\displaystyle|\phi(a+b)-\phi(a)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\prime}(a+ub)b\,du\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\phi^{\prime}(a)b|+\int_{0}^{1}|b|\cdot\left|\phi^{\prime}(a+ub)-\phi^{\prime}(a)\right|du

\displaystyle\leqslant|\phi^{\prime}(0)b|+|\phi^{\prime}(a)-\phi^{\prime}(0)|\cdot|b|+|b|\int_{0}^{1}c_{\phi}|ub|^{p-1}du

\displaystyle\leqslant|\phi^{\prime}(0)|\cdot|b|+c_{\phi}|a|^{p-1}\cdot|b|+\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}|b|^{p}.

(C.9)

We are also going to need a bound on
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left\{\max{\left\{|X_{s}|,|X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})|\right\}}\right\}
. Using (
C.6
), we find the following:

\displaystyle\max{\left\{|X_{s}|,|X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})|\right\}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]}m\mathbbm{1}_{\{x\in U_{m}\}}\eta(dx,dt)+\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}m\mathbbm{1}_{\{x\in U_{m}\}}\lambda(dx)dt

\displaystyle\leqslant m\left(\eta(U_{m}\times[0,1])+\lambda(U_{m})\right)

(C.10)

Using (
C.9
) with
a=0
and
b=\delta(h_{m})
, we get for the LHS in (
C.8
)

\mathbb{E}\left|\phi(\delta(h_{m}))\right|\leqslant|\phi^{\prime}(0)|\cdot\mathbb{E}|\delta(h_{m})|+\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\mathbb{E}|\delta(h_{m})|^{p}<\infty

(C.11)

which is finite since
\delta(h_{m})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
. To show that the first term on the RHS in (
C.8
) is integrable, we first apply Mecke formula (
2.6
) to get

\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]}\left(\phi(X_{s-}+h_{m}(\eta-\delta_{(y,s)},y,s))-\phi(X_{s-})\right)\eta(dy,ds)\right|\\
\leqslant\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})+h_{m}(\eta,y,s))-\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)}))\right|\lambda(dy)ds.

(C.12)

Now we combine (
C.9
), (
C.10
) and (
C.6
) to get that the RHS of (
C.12
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg(|\phi^{\prime}(0)|\cdot m\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\in U_{m}\}}+c_{\phi}|m\left(\eta(U_{m}\times[0,1])+\lambda(U_{m})\right)|^{p-1}\cdot m\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\in U_{m}\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 207.0021pt+\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}m^{p}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\in U_{m}\}}\bigg)\lambda(dy)ds

\displaystyle\leqslant m\lambda(U_{m})\left(|\phi^{\prime}(0)|+c_{\phi}m^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\eta(U_{m}\times[0,1])+\lambda(U_{m})\right)^{p-1}\right]+\frac{c_{\phi}m^{p-1}}{p}\right)<\infty

(C.13)

which is finite since
\eta(U_{m}\times[0,1])
is a Poisson random variable with parameter
\lambda(U_{m})
and thus all its moments are finite. This also shows that
\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})+h_{m}(\eta,y,s))-\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)}))\in L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
. The second term on the RHS can be treated by the same method.

We can now take expectations on both sides of (
C.8
) and apply Mecke formula (
2.6
) to get:

\mathbb{E}\phi(\delta(h_{m}))=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})+h_{m}(\eta,y,s))-\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)}))\lambda(dy)ds\\
-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\prime}(X_{s}(\eta))h_{m}(\eta,y,s)\lambda(dy)ds

(C.14)

Now add and subtract the integral of
\phi(X_{s-}(\eta)+h_{m}(\eta,y,s))-\phi(X_{s-}(\eta))
(which can be shown to be in
L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
by the same methods as above). We obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\phi(\delta(h_{m}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})+h_{m}(\eta,y,s))-\phi(X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)}))

\displaystyle\hskip 103.50105pt-\phi(X_{s-}(\eta)+h_{m}(\eta,y,s))+\phi(X_{s-}(\eta))\lambda(dy)ds

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\phi(X_{s-}(\eta)+h_{m}(\eta,y,s))-\phi(X_{s-}(\eta))-\phi^{\prime}(X_{s}(\eta))h_{m}(\eta,y,s)\lambda(dy)ds

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

(C.15)

To deal with
I_{1}
, note that for
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle|\phi(a+c)-\phi(a)-\phi(b+c)+\phi(b)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{a}^{b}\phi^{\prime}(u+c)-\phi^{\prime}(u)du\right|

(C.16)

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{a\wedge b}^{a\vee b}c_{\phi}|c|^{p-1}du

(C.17)

\displaystyle=c_{\phi}|a-b|\cdot|c|^{p-1}.

(C.18)

Hence

|I_{1}|\leqslant c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})-X_{s-}(\eta)\right|\cdot|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}\lambda(dy)ds.

(C.19)

Now we bound and rewrite part of the integrand on the RHS of (
C.19
) to find

\displaystyle\left|X_{s-}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)})-X_{s-}(\eta)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,s)}h_{m}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)}-\delta_{(x,t)},x,t)\eta(dx,dt)-\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{s}h_{m}(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)},x,t)\lambda(dx)dt\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\left.-\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,s)}h_{m}(\eta-\delta_{(x,t)},x,t)\eta(dx,dt)+\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{s}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\lambda(dx)dt\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,s)}\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta-\delta_{(x,t)},x,t)\eta(dx,dt)-\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{s}\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\lambda(dx)dt\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,s)}\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta-\delta_{(x,t)},x,t)\right|\eta(dx,dt)+\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{s}\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\right|\lambda(dx)dt

(C.20)

Multiplying this by
|h_{m}(\eta,x,t)|^{p-1}
and taking expectations, after an application of Mecke formula (
2.6
) we deduce that

|I_{1}|\leqslant c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\,\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\right|\\
\left(|h_{m}(\eta+\delta_{(x,t)},y,s)|^{p-1}+|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}\right)\lambda(dx)dt\lambda(dy)ds.

(C.21)

Since
|a-b|^{p-1}\leqslant|a|^{p-1}+|b|^{p-1}
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, one has that

|h_{m}(\eta+\delta_{(x,t)},y,s)|^{p-1}\leqslant|\MalD_{(x,t)}h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}+|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}

(C.22)

This implies that

\displaystyle|I_{1}|

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\right|\cdot|\MalD_{(x,t)}h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+2c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\,\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\right|\cdot|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}.

(C.23)

Now we consider
|I_{2}|
. For this, note that for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle|\phi(a+b)-\phi(a)-\phi^{\prime}(a)b|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{b}\phi^{\prime}(a+u)-\phi^{\prime}(a)du\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{\phi}\int_{0}^{|b|}u^{p-1}du

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}|b|^{p}.

(C.24)

Applying this to
a=X_{s}(\eta)
and
h=h_{m}(\eta,y,s)
yields

|I_{2}|\leqslant\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p}\lambda(dy)ds.

(C.25)

Observe that in the previous computation we implicitly used the fact that,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., the set
\left\{s\in[0,1]:X_{s}(\eta)\neq X_{s-}(\eta)\right\}
has zero Lebesgue measure.
We have therefore shown the following inequality:

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}\phi(\delta(h_{m}))|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\,|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\,\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\right|\cdot|\MalD_{(x,t)}h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+2c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\,\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h_{m}(\eta,x,t)\right|\cdot|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}.

(C.26)

By the construction of
h_{m}
, the RHS of this inequality is upper bounded by

\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\,|h(\eta,y,s)|^{p}\\
+c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h(\eta,x,t)\right|\cdot|\MalD_{(x,t)}h(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}\\
+2c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\,\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h(\eta,x,t)\right|\cdot|h(\eta,y,s)|^{p-1}.

(C.27)

In order to conclude the proof, it remains to show that
\mathbb{E}\phi(\delta(h_{m}))\rightarrow\mathbb{E}\phi(\delta(h))
, as
m\rightarrow\infty
. For this, we use (
C.9
) with
a=\delta(h)
and
b=\delta(h_{m})-\delta(h)
to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\phi(\delta(h_{m}))-\phi(\delta(h))|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}|\delta(h_{m})-\delta(h)|\left(|\phi^{\prime}(0)|+c_{\phi}|\delta(h)|^{p-1}+\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}|\delta(h_{m})-\delta(h)|^{p-1}\right).

(C.28)

Using, in order, the Cauchy-Schwarz, Minkowski and Jensen inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\phi(\delta(h_{m}))-\phi(\delta(h))|

\displaystyle\hskip 34.5021pt\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\delta(h_{m})-\delta(h)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(|\phi^{\prime}(0)|+c_{\phi}|\delta(h)|^{p-1}+\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}|\delta(h_{m})-\delta(h)|^{p-1}\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.5021pt\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\delta(h_{m}-h)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\left(|\phi^{\prime}(0)|+c_{\phi}\mathbb{E}\left[|\delta(h)|^{2}\right]^{(p-1)/2}+\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\mathbb{E}\left[|\delta(h_{m}-h)|^{2}\right]^{(p-1)/2}\right).

(C.29)

By the isometry property (
A.4
) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we infer that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\delta(h)|^{2}\right]\leqslant\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}h(\eta,y,s)^{2}\lambda(dy)ds+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\MalD_{(x,t)}h(\eta,y,s)\right)^{2}\lambda(dy)ds\lambda(dx)ds

(C.30)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\delta(h_{m}-h)|^{2}\right]\leqslant\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}(h_{m}(\eta,y,s)-h(\eta,y,s))^{2}\lambda(dy)ds

\displaystyle\hskip 68.99895pt+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\MalD_{(x,t)}h_{m}(\eta,y,s)-\MalD_{(x,t)}h(\eta,y,s)\right)^{2}\lambda(dy)ds\lambda(dx)ds.

(C.31)

The RHS of (
C.30
) is finite because
h
satisfies (
2.12
), and the RHS of (
C.31
) tends to
0
as
m\rightarrow\infty
by dominated convergence and the assumption (
2.12
). This implies convergence to
0
on the RHS of (
C.29
).

To show that the inequality holds in particular for
\phi(x)=|x|^{p}
with
p\in(1,2]
, it suffices to note that by Lemma
C.1
, this function satisfies the conditions of the theorem. For
\phi(x)=|x|
, we define
h_{m}
as in (
C.5
). Then
h_{m}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])\cap L^{2}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])\cap\dom\delta
and the pathwise representation (
2.13
) holds. By the triangle inequality, we have

\mathbb{E}|\delta(h_{m})|\leqslant\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]}|h_{m}(\eta-\delta_{y,s},y,s)|\eta(dy,ds)+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}|h_{m}(\eta,y,s)|\lambda(dy)ds.

(C.32)

By Mecke formula (
2.6
) and the fact that
|h_{m}|\leqslant|h|
, inequality (
4.3
) follows with
h_{m}
on the LHS instead of
h
, and the second and third term on the RHS being zero. As we have shown before,
\delta(h_{m})\rightarrow\delta(h)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
as
m\rightarrow\infty
, hence the inequality follows for
h
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.3
.

Step 1.
We first prove the following slightly modified version of (
4.3
) which holds under the weaker assumption
h\in\dom\delta
:

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\phi(\delta(h))\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds|h(\eta,y,s)|^{p}

\displaystyle+c_{\phi}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h(\eta,x,t)\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{(x,t)}h(\eta,y,s)\right|^{p}\right)^{1-1/p}

\displaystyle+2c_{\phi}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h(\eta,x,t)\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left|h(\eta,y,s)\right|^{p}\right)^{1-1/p}.

(C.33)

For
h
satisfying (
2.12
), this is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.2
by applying Hölder inequality.

Now let
h\in L^{2}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
and for
\tau\in(0,1)
define
P_{\tau}h
. By Lemma
A.2
, we have that
P_{\tau}h
satisfies (
2.12
), and so inequality (
C.33
) holds when
h
is replaced by
P_{\tau}h
. Using (
A.13
) and (
A.14
), we deduce

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\phi(\delta(P_{\tau}h))-\phi(0)\right|

(C.34)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds|h(\eta,y,s)|^{p}

\displaystyle+c_{\phi}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h(\eta,x,t)\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{(x,t)}h(\eta,y,s)\right|^{p}\right)^{1-1/p}

\displaystyle+2c_{\phi}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}h(\eta,x,t)\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left|h(\eta,y,s)\right|^{p}\right)^{1-1/p}.

(C.35)

It remains to show that
\phi(\delta(P_{\tau}h))\rightarrow\phi(\delta(h))
in
L^{1}(\eta)
as
\tau\rightarrow 1
. Using arguments similar to the ones in the proof of Theorem
4.2
, one shows that

\mathbb{E}|\phi(\delta(P_{\tau}h))-\phi(\delta(h))|\\
\leqslant\lVert\delta(P_{\tau}h-h)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})}\left(|\phi^{\prime}(0)|+c_{\phi}\lVert\delta(h)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})}^{p-1}+\frac{c_{\phi}}{p}\lVert\delta(P_{\tau}h-h)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})}^{p-1}\right).

(C.36)

By the isometry property (
A.1
), the second moment
\mathbb{E}\delta(h)^{2}
is finite since
h\in\dom\delta
and Lemma
A.2
implies that
\lVert\delta(P_{\tau}h-h)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})}\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow 1
.

Step 2.
Let
\in L^{2}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
be predictable. Then
h\in\dom\delta
and (
C.33
) holds. If
t<s
, then by predictability of
h
one has
h(\eta+\delta_{(y,s)},x,t)=h(\eta,x,t)
and hence
\MalD_{(y,s)}h(\eta,x,t)=0
. It follows that the second and third terms on the RHS of (
C.33
) are 0. Inequality (
4.4
) ensues.

Step 3.
To prove (
4.5
) for
p\in(1,2]
, let
h(\eta,x,s):=\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{(x,s)}F|\eta_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,s)}]
. This is predictable in the sense of (
2.16
) (see Section
2
) and by the Clark-Ocône representation (
2.18
), one has that

F=\mathbb{E}F+\delta(h)\qquad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

(C.37)

Define
\phi(x):=|x+\mathbb{E}F|^{p}-|\mathbb{E}F|^{p}
. Then
\phi(0)=0
and the derivative
\phi^{\prime}
is
(p-1)
-Hölder continuous with Hölder constant
2^{2-p}
by Lemma
C.1
. Moreover, it holds that

\phi(\delta(h))=|F|^{p}-|\mathbb{E}F|^{p}\qquad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

(C.38)

We can now apply (
4.4
) for this choice of
\phi
and
h
and inequality (
4.5
) follows.

For
p=1
, assume the RHS of the inequality (
4.5
) to be finite (else there is nothing to prove). Then
h(\eta,x,s)=\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{(x,s)}F|\eta_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,s)}]\in\dom\delta\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
and hence

\delta(h)=\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]}h(\eta-\delta_{(x,s)},x,s)\eta(dx,ds)-\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}h(\eta,x,s)\lambda(dx)ds.

(C.39)

Using triangle inequality and Mecke formula, we deduce the chain of inequalities

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|F|-|\mathbb{E}F|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}|F-\mathbb{E}F|

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}|\delta(h)|

(C.40)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]}|h(\eta-\delta_{(x,t)},x,t)|\eta(dx,dt)+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}|h(\eta,x,t)|\lambda(dx)dt

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}|h(\eta,x,t)|\lambda(dx)dt,

(C.41)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.7
.

Let
(U_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}\subset\mathbb{X}
be an increasing sequence of subsets such that
\bigcup_{n\geqslant 1}U_{n}=\mathbb{X}
and
\lambda(U_{n})<\infty
. Define for
n\in\mathbb{N}

h_{n}(\eta,y,s):=\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\in U_{n}\}}[(h(\eta,y,s)\wedge n)\vee(-n)].

(C.42)

Clearly
h_{n}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])\cap L^{2}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
and we can define
P_{\tau}h_{n}
for
\tau\in(0,1)
. We will start by showing (
4.9
) for
P_{\tau}h_{n}
instead of
h
. Using the definition (
A.9
), one easily checks that
P_{\tau}h_{n}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
. By Lemma
A.2
, it also follows that
P_{\tau}h_{n}\in\dom\delta
and hence we can apply Lemma
A.1
and deduce that

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,s)\MalD_{(x,s)}G\lambda(dx)ds=\mathbb{E}\left[G\delta(P_{\tau}h_{n})\right].

(C.43)

This implies by Jensen’s inequality that for any
p\in[1,2]

\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,s)\MalD_{(x,s)}G\lambda(dx)ds\right|\leqslant c_{G}\left(\mathbb{E}|\delta(P_{\tau}h_{n})|^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

(C.44)

As by Lemma
A.2
the quantity
P_{\tau}h_{n}
also satisfies (
2.12
), we can apply Theorem
4.2
to
\mathbb{E}|\delta(P_{\tau}h_{n})|^{p}
with
\phi(x)=|x|^{p}
(which satisfies the required conditions by Lemma
C.1
). After a further application of Hölder inequality, this yields for
p\in(1,2]
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\delta(P_{\tau}h_{n})|^{p}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2-p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds|P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,y,s)|^{p}

\displaystyle+p2^{2-p}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,t)\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\MalD_{(x,t)}P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,y,s)\right|^{p}\right]^{1-1/p}

\displaystyle+p2^{3-p}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dy)\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lambda(dx)\int_{0}^{s}dt\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\MalD_{(y,s)}P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,t)\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left|P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,y,s)\right|^{p}\right]^{1-1/p}.

(C.45)

Using Lemma
A.2
and the definition of
h_{n}
, we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\MalD_{(x,s)}P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,y,u)|^{p}\leqslant\mathbb{E}|\MalD_{(x,s)}h(\eta,y,u)|^{p}

(C.46)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,y,u)|^{p}\leqslant\mathbb{E}|h(\eta,y,u)|^{p}

(C.47)

hence (
C.45
) is upper bounded by the RHS of (
4.9
). For
p=1
, we reason as in the proof of Corollary
4.3
to deduce that

\mathbb{E}|\delta(P_{\tau}h_{n})|\leqslant 2\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}|P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,s)|\lambda(dx)ds,

(C.48)

which is again upper bounded by the RHS of (
4.9
).

It remains to take
\tau\rightarrow 1
and
n\rightarrow\infty
in the LHS of (
C.44
). By (
A.9
), one sees that
P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,s)=\mathbbm{1}_{\{x\in U_{n}\}}P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,s)
, and hence by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|P_{\tau}h_{n}(\eta,x,s)-h_{n}(\eta,x,s)\right|\cdot|\MalD_{(x,s)}G|\lambda(dx)ds\leqslant 2c_{G}\lambda(U_{n})\lVert P_{\tau}h_{n}-h_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])},

(C.49)

which converges to zero as
\tau\rightarrow 1
, by Lemma
A.2
. Therefore inequality (
4.9
) holds with
h_{n}
instead of
h
on the LHS. By dominated convergence,
h_{n}\MalD G\rightarrow h\MalD G
in
L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{X}\times[0,1])
, thus inequality (
4.9
) holds for
h
.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of Theorems
3.2
,
3.3
and
3.4

Throughout this section, we work with the simplified notation adopted in Remark
3.1
; recall also the definitions of the distances
d_{W}
and
d_{K}
given in (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), and write
\mathcal{H}
to denote the set of Lipschitz-continuous functions
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with Lipschitz constant
\lVert h\rVert_{L}\leqslant 1
. Let
N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Given
h\in\mathcal{H}
and
\phi(z)=\mathbb{P}(N\leqslant z)
, Stein’s equation

f^{\prime}(x)=xf(x)+h(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(y)\phi^{\prime}(y)dy,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

(D.1)

admits a
canonical solution

f_{h}
satisfying the two properties: (a)
f_{h}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\lVert f_{h}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}
and (b)
f_{h}^{\prime}
is Lipschitz-continuous with
\lVert f_{h}^{\prime}\rVert_{L}\leqslant 2
, see e.g.
[
PR16
, Theorem 3, Chapter 6]
and the references therein. In particular, one has that

d_{W}(F,N)=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}|f_{h}^{\prime}(F)-Ff_{h}(F)|.

(D.2)

Similarly, for fixed
z\in\mathbb{R}
, the canonical solution
f_{z}
to Stein’s equation

f_{z}^{\prime}(x)=xf_{z}(x)+\mathbbm{1}_{(-\infty,z]}(x)-\phi(z),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

(D.3)

is differentiable everywhere except at
z
, where it is customary to define
f_{z}^{\prime}(z)=zf_{z}(z)+1-\phi(z)
. One has that

•

\lVert f_{z}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{4}
and
\lVert f_{z}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant 1

•

|xf_{z}(x)|\leqslant 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and the function
x\mapsto xf_{z}(x)
is non-decreasing for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

(see e.g.
[
CGS11
, Lemma 2.3]
). We consequently have that

d_{K}(F,N)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}|f_{z}^{\prime}(F)-Ff_{z}(F)|.

(D.4)

Proof of Thm.
3.2
.

We will show the upper bounds in (
3.3
) and (
3.2
) by exploiting the representations (
D.2
) and (
D.4
). The proof is divided into three steps. First, we are going to derive the first terms on the RHSs of (
3.3
) and (
3.2
) for both Wasserstein and Kolmogorov distances at the same time. Second, we deduce the second term on the RHS of (
3.3
) and, as a last step, we find the second term on the RHS of (
3.2
). Throughout the proof, we fix
h\in\mathcal{H}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the corresponding canonical solutions
f_{h}
and
f_{z}
.

Step 1.
Write
f
for either
f_{h}
or
f_{z}
. Then,
f
is Lipschitz and there is a version of
f^{\prime}
which is bounded. Since
F\in\dom\MalD
and
f^{\prime}
is bounded, the expression

\mathbb{E}f^{\prime}(F)\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\ \mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)

(D.5)

is well-defined. Add and subtract this term to
\mathbb{E}f^{\prime}(F)-\mathbb{E}Ff(F)
and bound the resulting first term as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}f^{\prime}(F)-\mathbb{E}f^{\prime}(F)\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\ \mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}|f^{\prime}(F)|\cdot\left|1-\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\ \mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert f^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\,\mathbb{E}\left|1-\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\ \mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|.

(D.6)

As
\lVert f_{h}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}
and
\lVert f_{z}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant 1
, the bounds follow.

We are left to deal with

\left|\mathbb{E}Ff(F)-\mathbb{E}f^{\prime}(F)\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\ \mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|.

(D.7)

Since
f
is Lipschitz and
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
, it follows that
f(F)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
. Hence by Lemma
A.3

\mathbb{E}Ff(F)=\cov(F,f(F))=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\ \mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}f(F)|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.8)

Again by Lipschitzianity of
f
, it follows that
|\MalD_{y}f(F)|\leqslant|\MalD_{y}F|
and hence an application of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality justifies that

\mathbb{E}Ff(F)=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\MalD_{y}f(F)\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.9)

Therefore we are left to bound

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\cdot\left|f^{\prime}(F)\MalD_{y}F-\MalD_{y}f(F)\right|\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.10)

Step 2.
To bound (
D.10
) for the Wasserstein distance, we use an argument borrowed from the proof of
[
BOPT20
, Theorem 3.1]
to upper bound
|f_{h}(b)-f_{h}(a)-f_{h}^{\prime}(a)(b-a)|
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then by the properties stated above,
f_{h}
is Lipschitz and hence

|f_{h}(b)-f_{h}(a)-f_{h}^{\prime}(a)(b-a)|\leqslant|f_{h}(b)-f_{h}(a)|+|f_{h}^{\prime}(a)(b-a)|\leqslant 2\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{\pi}}|b-a|.

(D.11)

But at the same time by Lipschitzianity of
f_{h}^{\prime}
,

|f_{h}(b)-f_{h}(a)-f_{h}^{\prime}(a)(b-a)|=\left|\int_{a}^{b}f_{h}^{\prime}(x)-f_{h}^{\prime}(a)dx\right|\leqslant 2(b-a)^{2}.

(D.12)

We deduce that for any
q\in[1,2]

|f_{h}(b)-f_{h}(a)-f_{h}^{\prime}(a)(b-a)|\leqslant 2\min\{|b-a|,(b-a)^{2}\}\leqslant 2|b-a|^{q}.

(D.13)

It follows that

\displaystyle\left|f_{h}^{\prime}(F)\MalD_{y}F-\MalD_{y}f_{h}(F)\right|

\displaystyle=|f_{h}(F(\eta+\delta_{y}))-f_{h}(F(\eta))-f_{h}^{\prime}(F(\eta))(F(\eta+\delta_{y})-F(\eta))|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}.

(D.14)

and therefore (
D.10
) is bounded by

2\,\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right||\MalD_{y}F|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.15)

The required bound in the Wasserstein distance follows suit.

Step 3.
We reason as in the proof of
[
LRPY20
, Theorem 1.12]
to conclude

\left|f_{z}^{\prime}(F)\MalD_{y}F-\MalD_{y}f_{z}(F)\right|\leqslant\MalD_{y}F\cdot\MalD_{y}(Ff_{z}(F)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{F>z\}})

(D.16)

Thus (
D.10
) is upper bounded by

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\MalD_{y}F\cdot\MalD_{y}\left(Ff_{z}(F)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{F>z\}}\right)\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.17)

The desired bound now follows by taking the supremum over all
z\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

The proof will be split into two steps.

Step 1.
We start by showing that under the conditions of the theorem,

\mathbb{E}\left|1-\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\,\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|\leqslant\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}.

(D.18)

We can assume that
\mathbb{E}F=0
and
\sigma=1
(indeed, the result then follows since
\MalD\hat{F}=\sigma^{-1}\MalD F
).

For ease of notation, define

G:=\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\,\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)-1.

(D.19)

As a first step, we show that
\mathbb{E}G=0
.
As
F\in\dom\MalD
, we can use Fubini’s theorem and Lemma
A.3
to deduce

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\,\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]^{2}\bar{\lambda}(dy)=\var(F)=1.

(D.20)

Now by the modification (
4.6
) of Corollary
4.3
given in Remark
4.4
and since
G\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
, we have for
p\in[1,2]
,

\mathbb{E}|G|\leqslant\left(\mathbb{E}|G|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leqslant\left(2^{2-p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}[|\MalD_{x}G||\eta_{x}]^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}.

(D.21)

Let us now study the term

\MalD_{x}G=\MalD_{x}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\,\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)

(D.22)

and define

h(\eta,y):=\MalD_{y}F\,\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}].

(D.23)

We want to show that since
h\in L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{Y})
,

\left|\MalD_{x}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}h(\eta,y)\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)\right|\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.24)

and to do so we need an argument taken from the proof of
[
LPS16
, Proposition 4.1]
: Assume the RHS of (
D.24
) to be finite (if it is not, then the inequality (
D.24
) is trivially true). Then

\int_{\mathbb{Y}}|h(\eta+\delta_{x},y)|\bar{\lambda}(dy)\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)\right|+|h(\eta,y)|\bar{\lambda}(dy)<\infty

(D.25)

and hence

\MalD_{x}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}h(\eta,y)\bar{\lambda}(dy)=\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.26)

The inequality (
D.24
) follows. We have therefore shown

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|G|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(2^{2-p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\int_{\mathbb{Y}}|\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)|\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|\eta_{x}\right]^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\left(2^{2-p}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)|\big|\eta_{x}\big]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p},

(D.27)

where the second line follows from Tonelli’s theorem. By Minkowski’s integral inequality,

\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)|\big|\eta_{x}\big]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\big]^{1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}.

(D.28)

By the formula (
2.4
) for products,

\displaystyle\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)

\displaystyle=\MalD_{x}\left(\MalD_{y}F\,\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right)

\displaystyle=\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]+\MalD_{y}F\cdot\MalD_{x}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]+\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot\MalD_{x}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}].

(D.29)

Since
\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]
depends on
\eta
only through
\eta_{y}
, it follows that
\MalD_{x}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]=0
whenever
x\geqslant y
. Moreover, since
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
implies that
\MalD_{y}F,\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
by
[
LP11b
, Theorem 1.1]
, if
x<y
, we can put the add-one cost operator inside the conditional expectation. Therefore

\MalD_{x}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]=\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{y}].

(D.30)

By Minkowski’s norm inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{x}h(\eta,y)|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{y}F\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{y}]|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{y}]|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}.

(D.31)

By the properties of conditional expectations and splitting the first term into two parts, (
D.31
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\leqslant x\}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathbb{E}[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F||\eta_{x}]^{p}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F||\eta_{y}]\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[|\MalD_{y}F||\eta_{y}]\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{y}]|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F||\eta_{y}]\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{y}]|\big|\eta_{x}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}.

(D.32)

By an application of Jensen’s inequality (
D.32
) is now bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|^{p}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\big|\eta_{x\vee y}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{y}F|\big|\eta_{y}\big]^{p}\cdot\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\big|\eta_{y}\big]\big|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\big|\eta_{y}\big]^{2p}\right]^{1/p}.

(D.33)

Plugging the conclusion from (
D.28
) - (
D.33
) into (
D.27
) and applying Minkowski’s norm inequality again yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|G|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2/p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\big|^{p}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\big|\eta_{x\vee y}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+2^{2/p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{y}F|\big|\eta_{y}\big]^{p}\cdot\big|\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{y}]\big|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant}+2^{2/p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\big|\eta_{y}\big]^{2p}\right]^{1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

(D.34)

which is in turn bounded by

\displaystyle 2^{2/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{y}F|\big|\eta_{y}\big]^{p}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\big|\eta_{x\vee y}\big]^{p}\right]^{1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+2^{2/p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\big|\eta_{y}\big]^{2p}\right]^{1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right)^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}.

(D.35)

The result follows by an application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Step 2.
We want to show that

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\cdot|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\leqslant\beta_{3}+\beta_{4}.

(D.36)

Again it suffices to show (
D.36
) for
F
with
\mathbb{E}F=0
and
\mathbb{E}F^{2}=1
. Assume the
\beta_{3},\beta_{4}
to be finite (otherwise there is nothing to prove). Then, in particular

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\MalD_{y}F\right|\eta_{y}\right]\right|^{q+1}\bar{\lambda}(dy)<\infty

(D.37)

and we can add and subtract this term to the LHS of (
D.36
), yielding
\beta_{3}
and the following rest term:

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\cdot|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dy)-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\MalD_{y}F\right|\eta_{y}\right]\right|^{q+1}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\cdot\mathbb{E}[|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\MalD_{y}F\right|\eta_{y}\right]\right|^{q+1}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|\cdot\big|\mathbb{E}[|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}|\eta_{y}]-\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\MalD_{y}F\right|\eta_{y}\right]\right|^{q}\big|\bar{\lambda}(dy),

(D.38)

where the equality is justified by the fact that
\mathbb{E}[G|\eta_{y}]
is defined for all
G\in L^{0}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
which are integrable or non-negative (see the definition (
2.17
)).

The term
\mathbb{E}[|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}|\eta_{y}]-\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\MalD_{y}F\right|\eta_{y}\right]\right|^{q}
can be rewritten as

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}|g_{y}(\chi)|^{q}-|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}g_{y}(\chi)|^{q},

(D.39)

where
g_{y}(\chi)=\MalD_{y}F(\eta_{y}+\chi)
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
is the expectation with respect to
\chi
, a Poisson measure on the space
\{x\in\mathbb{Y}:x\geqslant y\}
with intensity
\lambda\otimes ds
, independent of
\eta
. As
F\in\dom\MalD
, it can be shown that
g_{y}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\chi})
for a.e.
y\in\mathbb{Y}
.

By (
4.5
) in Corollary
4.3
,

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}|g_{y}(\chi)|^{q}-|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}g_{y}(\chi)|^{q}\leqslant 2^{2-q}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\leqslant x\}}\big|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\MalD_{x}g_{y}(\chi)|\chi_{x}]\big|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dx).

(D.40)

Plugging (
D.40
) into (
D.38
), we deduce that the RHS of (
D.38
) is upper bounded by

2^{2-q}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\leqslant x\}}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\big|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\MalD_{x}g_{y}(\chi)|\chi_{x}]\big|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.41)

Since
\chi
and
\eta
are independent and have the same intensity measure,

\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\leqslant x\}}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|\cdot\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\big|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\MalD_{x}g_{y}(\chi)|\chi_{x}]\big|^{q}

(D.42)

has the same law as

\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\leqslant x\}}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[|\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{x}]|^{p}\big|\eta_{y}\big].

(D.43)

This implies finally that the RHS of (
D.38
) is upper bounded by

2^{2-q}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\leqslant x\}}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{x}]|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dx)\bar{\lambda}(dy)

(D.44)

and the result follows by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

It suffices to prove this result for
F
such that
\mathbb{E}F=0
and
\sigma=1
. If Theorem
3.4
holds for all
\sigma
-finite spaces
\mathbb{X}
, it holds in particular for the space
\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]
. In fact, it suffices to prove Theorem
3.4
for functionals
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})\cap\dom\MalD
, where
\eta
is a
(\mathbb{X}\times[0,1],\lambda\otimes ds)
-Poisson measure. Indeed, as discussed in Section
2
, we can regard any functional
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\chi})
, with the
(\mathbb{X},\lambda)
-Poisson measure
\chi
, as a functional of
\eta
without changing the law of
F
or its add-one costs. If we replace
\mathbb{X}
by
\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]
and
\chi
by
\eta
in the terms
\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{7}
, the expressions do not change either since for any
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\chi})\cap\dom\MalD
, the integrands do not depend on time. For the rest of this proof, we let thus
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})\cap\dom\MalD
and recall the notation from Remark
3.1
where
\mathbb{Y}:=\mathbb{X}\times[0,1]
and
\bar{\lambda}:=\lambda\otimes ds
are explicitly defined.

We divide this proof into two steps: first, we discuss the bound on the Wasserstein distance, second, we show the bound on the Kolmogorov distance.

Step 1.
By a combination of Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
, we see that

\displaystyle d_{W}(G,N)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\mathbb{E}\left|1-\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\MalD_{y}F\,\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right|+2\,\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\cdot|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dy)

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+2\,\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\cdot|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.45)

To bound
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
, we simply apply Jensen’s inequality and bound
\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}
by
1
. This gives

\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\leqslant\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}.

(D.46)

For the second term, apply Hölder’s inequality to deduce

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|\cdot|\MalD_{y}F|^{q}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]\right|^{q+1}\right)^{1/(q+1)}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}|\MalD_{y}F|^{q+1}\right)^{1-1/(q+1)}\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.47)

A further application of Jensen’s inequality yields the result.

Step 2.
We start from inequality (
3.2
) in Theorem
3.2
. The first term was dealt with in Step 1 of this proof. Let us deal with the second term. Define

h(\eta,y):=\MalD_{y}F\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|

(D.48)

and for
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

Z_{z}:=Ff_{z}(F)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{F>z\}}.

(D.49)

The term we want to bound is thus given by

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}h(\eta,y)\MalD_{y}Z_{z}\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.50)

Since
F\in\dom\MalD
, it follows by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that
h\in L^{1}(\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{Y})
. Moreover, by the properties of
f_{z}
, we have
|Z_{z}|\leqslant 2
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence we can apply Corollary
4.7
and get for any
p\in[1,2]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}h(\eta,y)\MalD_{y}Z_{z}\bar{\lambda}(dy)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\left(2^{2-p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}|h(\eta,y)|^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\right.

\displaystyle+p2^{2-p}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\MalD_{y}h(\eta,x)\right|^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)

\displaystyle+\left.p2^{3-p}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{y}h(\eta,x)\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left|h(\eta,y)\right|^{p}\right)^{1-1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leqslant(I_{1})^{1/p}+(I_{2})^{1/p}+(I_{3})^{1/p},

(D.51)

since
|a+b|^{1/p}\leqslant|a|^{1/p}+|b|^{1/p}
.

Let us first look at
I_{1}
. By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen inequalities, it follows that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=4\,\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}|\MalD_{y}F|^{p}\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{y}F|\eta_{y}]|^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{y}F|^{2p}\right]\bar{\lambda}(dy).

(D.52)

Now we deal with
I_{2}
. By the formula (
2.4
),

\MalD_{y}h(\eta,x)=\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot\MalD_{y}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|+\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|+\MalD_{x}F\cdot\MalD_{y}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|.

(D.53)

By Minkowski’s norm inequality,

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\MalD_{y}h(\eta,x)\right|^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot\MalD_{y}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|\right|^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|\right|^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\big|\MalD_{x}F\cdot\MalD_{y}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|\big|^{p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}.

(D.54)

By the triangle inequality, and as in the proof of Theorem
3.3
,

\big|\MalD_{y}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|\big|\leqslant\mathbbm{1}_{\{y<x\}}|\MalD_{y}\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|\leqslant|\mathbb{E}[\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|\eta_{x}]|.

(D.55)

By the Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen inequalities,

\displaystyle(I_{2})^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leqslant(4p)^{1/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|^{2p}\right]\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+2^{2/p+1}p^{1/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|^{2p}\right]^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{x}F|^{2p}\right]^{1/2}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx)\right)^{1/p}.

(D.56)

Lastly, we look at the term
I_{3}
. Since
\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]
depends only on
\eta_{x}
, we get

\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\MalD_{y}|\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]|=0.

(D.57)

Hence

\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\big(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{y}h(\eta,x)\right|^{p}\big)^{1/p}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<y\}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F\cdot\mathbb{E}[\MalD_{x}F|\eta_{x}]\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

(D.58)

Applying Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen again yields

I_{3}\leqslant 8p\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\int_{\mathbb{Y}}\left(\mathbb{E}|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{2p}}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}|\MalD_{x}F|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{2p}}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\MalD_{y}F\right|^{2p}\right)^{1-1/p}\bar{\lambda}(dy)\bar{\lambda}(dx).

(D.59)

This inequality concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Online Nearest Neighbour Graph

The technical proofs in this section sometimes use ideas and techniques close to
[
Pen05
, Section 3.4]
and
[
Wad09
]
(and previous papers). We will discuss the connections with this part of the literature as the proofs unfold.

Throughout this section, we work under the setting of Section
5.1
. We will adopt the following notation: for a measurable subset
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we will write
\eta_{A}
for
\eta_{|A\times[0,1]}
. Moreover, we need to adapt the definition of ‘generic’ to sets in the marked space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]
. In this section only, we call a finite set
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]

generic
if

•

all projections of
\mu
onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are distinct;

•

all pairwise distances between projections of
\mu
onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are distinct;

•

all projections of
\mu
onto
[0,1]
are distinct.

Note that for any convex body
D_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the restriction of an
(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1],dx\otimes ds)
-Poisson measure
\eta
to
D_{0}\times[0,1]
has generic support. We call
\mu

generic with respect to
a point (or points)
(x,s),(y,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]
if
\mu\cup\{(x,s),(y,u)\}
is generic.

We start with a short discussion of the properties of the space
H
defined in Section
5.1
. Since
H
has non-empty interior, there exist
\delta>0
and
y_{0}\in H
such that
B^{d}(y_{0},\delta)\subset H
. Fix these
\delta
and
y_{0}
throughout this section. For
\epsilon>0
define

H_{\epsilon}:=\{x\in H:\dist(x,\partial H)>\epsilon\},

(E.1)

where
\dist
denotes the Euclidean distance and
\partial H
the boundary of
H
. For small
\epsilon
, this set is non-empty. By
[
HLS16
, (3.19)]
, one has

|H\setminus H_{\epsilon}|\leqslant|H+B^{d}(0,\epsilon)|-|H|,

(E.2)

where the sum is the Minkowski sum of sets. By Steiner’s formula (cf.
[
SW08
, (14.5)]
), it follows that there is a constant
\beta_{H}>0
such that

|H\setminus H_{\epsilon}|\leqslant\beta_{H}\,\epsilon.

(E.3)

E.1
Moment estimates of conditional expectations of add-one costs

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following proposition:

Proposition E.1
.

Assume the conditions in the statement of Theorem
5.3
. Let
\alpha>0
and
r\geqslant 1
. Then for all
t\geqslant 1
and all
(x,s),(y,u)\in tH\times(0,1]
with
s<u
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s)}\big]^{r}\right]^{1/r}\leqslant c_{1}(s^{-\alpha/d}\wedge t^{\alpha})

(E.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,u)}\big]^{r}\right]^{1/r}\leqslant c_{1}(u^{-\alpha/d}\wedge t^{\alpha}).

(E.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,u)}\big]\neq 0\right)\leqslant C_{2}\exp(-c_{2}u|x-y|^{d}).

(E.6)

where
c_{1}>0
is a constant depending on
\alpha,r
and
\epsilon
and
c_{2},C_{2}>0
depend only on
d
and
H
.

Remark E.2
.

The bound (
E.4
) can be compared to Lemma 4.2 in
[
Wad09
]
, with the difference that we work in a Poisson setting and consider general moments
r\geqslant 1
, whereas
[
Wad09
]
considers uniform random variables and
r=2
.

We start with two technical lemmas.

Lemma E.3
.

Let
\epsilon,\ell>0
. There exists
0<q<1
depending only on
\ell,\epsilon
and
d
such that for every convex body
D_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
\diam(D_{0})\leqslant\ell
and
B^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)\subset D_{0}
for some
y_{0}\in D_{0}
, for every
w\in D_{0}
and
0<s\leqslant\ell
, there is a point
w^{\prime}\in D_{0}
with

B^{d}(w^{\prime},qs)\subset D_{0}\cap B^{d}(w,s).

(E.7)

In particular, this implies that

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in D_{0}\\
0<s\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\frac{|D_{0}\cap B^{d}(w,s)|}{s^{d}}\geqslant c_{1}(\epsilon,\ell,d)

(E.8)

with
c_{1}(\epsilon,\ell,d)>0
a constant depending on
\epsilon,\ell
and
d
.

In words, this lemma means that there is a constant rate
q
such that for any not-too-big ball intersecting
D_{0}
, we can find a smaller ball shrunken by the factor
q
within the intersection. The ratio
q
at which the ball is shrunk depends only on a lower bound for the in-radius of
D_{0}
and an upper bound for the diameter of
D_{0}
. This will become important in the proof of Proposition
E.1
, where
D_{0}
is a probabilistic object, but has deterministic upper and lower bounds.

Remark E.4
.

While geometric lemmas in the spirit of Lemma
E.3
are already shown in
[
Pen05
,
Wad09
]
and appear in some form in many papers on stochastic geometry, we need a version that allows for fine control over the constants, in order to be able to state our results in the full generality of convex sets.

Proof.

Since the ball
B^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)
is a subset of
D_{0}
and
D_{0}
is closed and convex, for any
w\in D_{0}
, the convex hull of
w\cup\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)
is inside
D_{0}
. If
d\geqslant 2
and for
w\notin\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)
, this is a cone-like structure with angular radius
\omega_{w}=\arcsin(\epsilon/|w-y_{0}|)
. Since
\diam(D_{0})\leqslant\ell
, it follows that

\frac{\pi}{4}\wedge\inf_{w\in D_{0}}\omega_{w}\geqslant\frac{\pi}{4}\wedge\arcsin\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\ell}\right)=:\theta.

(E.9)

For every
w\in D_{0}
, let
C(w)
be the closed cone centred at
w
, of angular radius
\theta
and central axis the half-line from
w
through
y_{0}
(if
d=1
, take
C(w)
to be the closed half-line starting at
w
and containing
y_{0}
). The set
A(w,\epsilon):=C(w)\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(w,\epsilon)
is now included in
D_{0}
. This is clear if
d=1
. If
d\geqslant 2
, then if
w\notin\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)
, it follows that
|y_{0}-w|>\epsilon
and since
\theta\leqslant\omega_{w}\wedge\frac{\pi}{4}
, one deduces that
A(w,\epsilon)
is inside the convex hull of
w\cup\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)
(here we need
\theta\leqslant\frac{\pi}{4}
). If
w\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)
, then by construction
A(w,\epsilon)
is also inside
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(y_{0},\epsilon)
(here we need
\theta\leqslant\frac{\pi}{3}
).

Since
s,\epsilon\leqslant\ell
, it follows that

A\left(w,\frac{\epsilon s}{\ell}\right)\subset A(w,\epsilon)\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(w,s)\subset D_{0}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(w,s).

(E.10)

The set
A(0,1)=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(0,1)\cap C(0)
is convex and of non-zero volume, therefore it contains an open ball of some radius
r>0
, centred at some point
z\in A(0,1)
. By translation and scaling,

B^{d}\left(\frac{\epsilon s}{\ell}\cdot z+w,\frac{\epsilon sr}{\ell}\right)\subset A\left(w,\frac{\epsilon s}{\ell}\right).

(E.11)

Thus with
q=\frac{\epsilon r}{\ell}
and
w^{\prime}=\frac{\epsilon s}{\ell}\cdot z+w
, we achieve

B^{d}(w^{\prime},qs)\subset A\left(w,\frac{\epsilon s}{\ell}\right)\subset D_{0}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(w,s).

(E.12)

Since
B^{d}(w^{\prime},qs)
is open, we have
B^{d}(w^{\prime},qs)\subset B^{d}(w,s)
.
The statement (
E.8
) follows easily by taking
c_{1}(\epsilon,\ell,d):=\kappa_{d}q^{d}
.
∎

For the next part, we need some additional technical definitions. Let
C_{1}(0),...,C_{K}(0)
be a collection of closed cones centred at
0
and of angular radius
\frac{\pi}{12}
such that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}C_{i}(0)=\mathbb{R}^{d}
(for
d=1
, take
C_{1}(0):=(-\infty,0]
and
C_{2}(0):=[0,\infty)
). Define
C_{i}(x):=C_{i}(0)+x
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
C_{1}^{+}(x),...,C_{K}^{+}(x)
be the cones centred at
x
of angular radius
\frac{\pi}{6}
such that
C_{i}^{+}(x)
has the same central half-axis as
C_{i}(x)
for each
i
(for
d=1
, take
C_{i}^{+}(0)=C_{i}(0)
).

For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
a convex body,
x\in A
and
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]
a finite set which is generic with respect to
x
, define

•

s_{i}(x,A):=\diam(C_{i}(x)\cap A)
;

•

R_{i,\theta}(x,A,\mu):=\inf\{|x-y|:\ (y,u)\in\left(A\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)\times[0,\theta)\right)\cap\mu\}\wedge s_{i}(x,A)
for
\theta\in[0,1]
and where
\inf\emptyset=\infty
;

•

R_{\theta}(x,A,\mu):=\max_{i=1,...,K}R_{i,\theta}(x,A,\mu)
.

In practice, when it is clear what
A
and
\mu
are, we will omit them from the notation and write
s_{i}(x),R_{i,\theta}(x),R_{\theta}(x)
. In words,
s_{i}(x,A)
is the maximal distance within
A
from
x
to any point in the cone
C_{i}(x)
. The quantity
R_{i,\theta}(x,A,\mu)
is the distance to the closest point of
\mu
of mark smaller then
\theta
within
A
and the larger cone
C_{i}^{+}(x)
. The quantity
R_{\theta}(x,A,\mu)
is such that the ball
B^{d}(x,R_{\theta}(x,A,\mu))
either contains a point of mark less than
\theta
, or if it doesn’t, then it contains all of
A
.

Remark E.5
.

The use of cones and the construction of the radii
R_{\theta}
follows the ideas given in
[
Pen05
,
Wad09
]
, again with the difference that we work in a Poisson setting without passing through uniform random vectors.

Lemma E.6
.

Let
\epsilon,\ell>0
and
D_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be as in Lemma
E.3
. Take
\lambda>0
and
x\in\lambda D_{0}
, and let
0<r\leqslant s_{i}(x,\lambda D_{0})
for some
i\in\{1,...,K\}
. Then there is a constant
c_{2}(\epsilon,\ell,d)>0
such that

|\lambda D_{0}\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)\cap B^{d}(x,r)|\geqslant c_{2}(\epsilon,\ell,d)r^{d}.

(E.13)

Remark E.7
.

A similar result was shown in the proof of
[
Wad09
, Lemma 3.2]
, with a constant depending on
D_{0}
.

Proof.

First, we show that there is a point
z\in\lambda D_{0}
such that
B^{d}(z,\frac{r}{8})\subset C_{i}^{+}(x)\cap B^{d}(x,r)
. Indeed, as
r\leqslant s_{i}(x,\lambda D_{0})
, there is by convexity a point
z\in\lambda D_{0}\cap C_{i}(x)
such that
|x-z|=\frac{r}{2}
. Now consider any point
y\in B^{d}(z,\frac{r}{8})
. For
d=1
, one has that
y\in C_{i}^{+}(x)\cap B^{1}(x,r)
. For
d\geqslant 2
, the angle
\angle zxy
will be largest when the line
(xy)
is tangent to
B^{d}(z,\frac{r}{8})
, in which case

\angle zxy\leqslant\arcsin\left(\frac{r/8}{r/2}\right)=\arcsin\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)<\frac{\pi}{12}.

(E.14)

Since
z\in C_{i}(x)
, this implies that
y\in C_{i}^{+}(x)
and we clearly also have
|x-y|\leqslant|x-z|+|z-y|\leqslant\frac{5}{8}r<r
. We infer that for all
d\geqslant 1
,

|\lambda D_{0}\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)\cap B^{d}(x,r)|\geqslant|\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(z,\tfrac{r}{8})|.

(E.15)

Now
\frac{1}{\lambda}z\in D_{0}
and
\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{r}{8}\leqslant\diam(D_{0})\leqslant\ell
, therefore Lemma
E.3
implies

\left|\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}\left(z,\tfrac{r}{8}\right)\right|=\lambda^{d}\left|D_{0}\cap B^{d}\left(\tfrac{1}{\lambda}z,\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\tfrac{r}{8}\right)\right|\geqslant\lambda^{d}c_{1}(\epsilon,\ell,d)\left(\tfrac{r}{8\lambda}\right)^{d}=8^{-d}c_{1}(\epsilon,\ell,d)r^{d},

(E.16)

which yields the desired bound.
∎

Lemma E.8
.

Let
\epsilon,\ell>0
and
D_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be as in Lemma
E.3
. Let
\lambda>0
,
x\in\lambda D_{0}
and
\theta\in(0,1)
. Then for all
\beta>0
, there is a constant
c_{3}(\epsilon,d,\ell,\beta)>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[R_{\theta}(x,\lambda D_{0},\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})^{\beta}\right]\leqslant c_{3}(\epsilon,d,\ell,\beta)\left(\theta^{-\beta/d}\wedge\lambda^{\beta}\right).

(E.17)

Remark E.9
.

This result is comparable to
[
Wad09
, Lemma 3.2]
and an argument in the proof of
[
Pen05
, Lemma 3.3]
, both of which worked with uniform random vectors.

Proof.

It is clear that by construction
R_{\theta}(x,\lambda D_{0},\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\leqslant\diam(\lambda D_{0})\leqslant\ell\lambda
, thus we need only show the bound by
\theta^{-\beta/d}
. To simplify the notation, write
R_{\theta}(x)
for
R_{\theta}(x,\lambda D_{0},\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})
. We are going to study the probability
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}_{\theta}(x)>r)
. If for some
r>0
, we have
R_{\theta}(x)>r
, then
\max_{i=1,...,K}R_{i,\theta}(x)>r
, implying that there is an
i\in\{1,...,K\}
such that
R_{i,\theta}(x)>r
. Since
R_{i,\theta}(x)\leqslant s_{i}(x)
, this implies in turn that
r<s_{i}(x)
and that

\eta\left(\left(\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(x,r)\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)\right)\times[0,\theta)\right)=0.

(E.18)

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}_{\theta}(x)>r)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}\left\{\eta\left((\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(x,r)\cap C_{i}^{+}(x))\times[0,\theta)\right)=0,\ r<s_{i}(x)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathbbm{1}_{\{r<s_{i}(x)\}}\mathbb{P}\left(\eta\left((\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(x,r)\cap C_{i}^{+}(x))\times[0,\theta)\right)=0\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathbbm{1}_{\{r<s_{i}(x)\}}\exp\left(-\theta|\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(x,r)\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)|\right).

(E.19)

By Lemma
E.6
, there is a constant
C:=c_{2}(\epsilon,\ell,d)>0
such that

|\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(x,r)\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)|\geqslant Cr^{d}.

(E.20)

Hence

\exp\left(-\theta|\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(x,r)\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)|\right)\leqslant\exp(-\theta Cr^{d})

(E.21)

and

\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}_{\theta}(x)>r)\leqslant K\exp(-\theta Cr^{d}).

(E.22)

We can now estimate for all
\beta>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[R_{\theta}(x)^{\beta}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(R_{\theta}(x)>r^{1/\beta})dr

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}K\exp(-\theta Cr^{d/\beta})dr

\displaystyle=K\frac{\beta}{d}\theta^{-\beta/d}C^{-\beta/d}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{\beta/d-1}\exp(-u)du

\displaystyle=KC^{-\beta/d}\Gamma\left(\frac{\beta}{d}+1\right)\theta^{-\beta/d},

(E.23)

by the properties of the Gamma function
\Gamma
(see e.g.
[
AS72
, 6.1.1]
).
∎

Before we pass to the next lemma, we note some useful properties of the add-one cost and introduce the quantity
\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)
. Let
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]
and
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]
be a generic set with respect to
(x,s)
. Note that

\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)=e(x,s,\mu)^{\alpha}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,u)\in\\
\mu\cap(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(s,1])\end{subarray}}\left(e(y,u,\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})^{\alpha}-e(y,u,\mu)^{\alpha}\right).

(E.24)

Define the quantity

\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,u)\in\\
\mu\cap(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(s,1])\end{subarray}}e(y,u,\mu)^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(y,u)\rightarrow(x,s)\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}\}},

(E.25)

where we use (as before) the notation
(y,u)\rightarrow(x,s)\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}
in order to indicate that the point
(y,u)
connects to
(x,s)
in the collection of points
\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}
, as was explained in Section
5.1
.

We claim the following: For any convex body
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the projection onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
\mu
is included in the interior of
A
, it holds that

\left|\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)\right|\leqslant R_{s}(x,A,\mu)^{\alpha}+\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu).

(E.26)

To show the claim, we start by noting the following: if a point
(y,u)\in\mu\cap(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(s,1])
has an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
, then
e(y,u,\mu)\neq 0
. There are now two possibilities:

1.

The point
(y,u)
connects to
(x,s)
in
\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}
. This implies that
e(y,u,\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})<e(y,u,\mu)
.

2.

The point
(y,u)
does not connect to
(x,s)
in
\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}
. Then
e(y,u,\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})=e(y,u,\mu)
.

In both cases, it holds that

|e(y,u,\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})^{\alpha}-e(y,u,\mu)^{\alpha}|\leqslant e(y,u,\mu)^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(y,u)\rightarrow(x,s)\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}\}}.

(E.27)

As a next step, consider three scenarios:

1.

If
\mu\cap{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,s)}\neq\emptyset
, then any point
(y,u)\in\mu
with
u>s
has an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
and (
E.27
) holds. Moreover, the point
(x,s)
has an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
and by construction,
e(x,s,\mu)\leqslant R_{s}(x,A,\mu)
. Combining this with (
E.24
) and (
E.27
) implies that (
E.26
) holds.

2.

If
\mu\cap{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,s)}=\emptyset
but
\mu\cap{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(s,1]}\neq\emptyset
, then the point
(x,s)
does not have an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
and
e(x,s,\mu)=0
. However, there is now a point
(y_{0},u_{0})\in\mu\cap{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(s,1]}
which is the point of lowest mark in
\mu
and it does not have an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
. Hence
e(y_{0},u_{0},\mu)=0
and
e(y_{0},u_{0},\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})=|x-y_{0}|
since this point will connect to
(x,s)
in
\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}
. Since
\mu\cap{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,s)}=\emptyset
, we have that
A\subset\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{s}(x,A,\mu))
and hence
|x-y_{0}|\leqslant R_{s}(x,A,\mu)
. Any point
(y,u)\in\mu
different from
(y_{0},u_{0})
must have an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
, since
(y_{0},u_{0})
is a potential neighbour. Thus for such
(y,u)
, the inequality (
E.27
) holds and we deduce

\displaystyle\left|\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)\right|

\displaystyle=\bigg||x-y_{0}|^{\alpha}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{0},u_{0})\neq(y,u)\in\\
\mu\cap(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(s,1])\end{subarray}}\left(e(y,u,\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})^{\alpha}-e(y,u,\mu)^{\alpha}\right)\bigg|

(E.28)

\displaystyle\leqslant R_{s}(x,A,\mu)^{\alpha}+\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu),

(E.29)

thus inequality (
E.26
) holds.

3.

If
\mu=\emptyset
, then
\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)=0
and inequality (
E.26
) trivially holds.

This concludes the proof of the claim. In the next lemma, we now provide a bound for the quantity
\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})
.

Lemma E.10
.

Let
\epsilon,\ell>0
and
D_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be as in Lemma
E.3
. Let
\lambda>0
and
(x,s)\in\lambda D_{0}\times[0,1]
. Then there is a constant
c_{3}(\epsilon,\ell,d,\alpha)>0
such that

\mathbb{E}[\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})]\leqslant c_{4}(\epsilon,\ell,d,\alpha)(s^{-\alpha/d}\wedge\lambda^{\alpha}).

(E.30)

Proof.

To prove (
E.30
), let
(y,u)\in\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}
be a point connecting to
(x,s)
in
\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}+\delta_{(x,s)}
. We will proceed in three steps.

Step 1.
We claim that
y\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,\mathbb{R}_{\theta}(x,\lambda D_{0},\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}))
for any
\theta<u
.

As the
C_{i}(x)
,
i=1,...,K
, cover
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there is an
i
such that
y\in C_{i}(x)
. Assume that
|x-y|>R_{\theta}(x)
. Since
|x-y|\leqslant s_{i}(x,\lambda D_{0})
, we must have

R_{i,\theta}(x)\leqslant R_{\theta}(x)<s_{i}(x,\lambda D_{0})

(E.31)

and hence

\eta\cap((\lambda D_{0}\cap C_{i}^{+}(x)\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{\theta}(x)))\times[0,\theta))\neq\emptyset.

(E.32)

So there is a point
(z,v)
within this set and in particular
v<\theta<u
. We now have:

•

|x-y|>R_{\theta}(x)\geqslant|x-z|

•

z\in C_{i}^{+}(x)

•

y\in C_{i}(x)
.

By the geometric properties of the cones
C_{i}(x)
and
C_{i}^{+}(x)
, shown in
[
Pen05
, Lemma 3.3]
, this implies that
|z-y|\leqslant|x-y|
. Since
|z-y|\neq|x-y|
a.s., we deduce that the point
y
will connect to
z
rather than to
x
, which is a contradiction. We must thus have
|x-y|\leqslant R_{\theta}(x)
.

Step 2.
We will now derive the first part of the bound in (
E.30
).

For
0\leqslant\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\leqslant 1
, define

H(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,u)\in\\
\eta\cap(\lambda D_{0}\times(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}])\end{subarray}}e(y,u,\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(y,u)\rightarrow(x,s)\text{ in }\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}+\delta_{(x,s)}\}}.

(E.33)

This is the contribution to the sum
\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})
of points with marks in the interval
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]
, so that

\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})=H(0,1)=H(s,1)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}),

(E.34)

for any partition
s=\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}<...<\theta_{n}=1
.

Let
(y,u)\in\eta\cap\left(\lambda D_{0}\times(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}]\right)
be a point connecting to
x
. By Step 1, we have
y\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x))
. By construction, either
\lambda D_{0}\cap B^{d}(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x))
contains a point
(w,v)
of
\eta
with mark less than
\theta_{i-1}
, in which case
e(y,u,\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\leqslant|y-w|\leqslant|x-y|+|x-w|
, or
\lambda D_{0}\subset B^{d}(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x))
. In both cases,

e(y,u,\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\leqslant|x-y|+R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)\leqslant 2R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x).

(E.35)

Moreover, the number of points in
\lambda D_{0}\times(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}]
connecting to
x
is upper bounded by the total number of points in
\eta\cap\left((\lambda D_{0}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)))\times(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}]\right)
.
From this we conclude that

H(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i})\leqslant 2^{\alpha}R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)^{\alpha}\eta\left((\lambda D_{0}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)))\times(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}]\right).

(E.36)

To upper bound the expectation of
H(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i})
, we are going to use the fact that
\eta_{|\lambda D_{0}\times[0,\theta_{i-1}]}
and
\eta_{|\lambda D_{0}\times(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}]}
are independent and
R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)
is measurable with respect to
\eta_{|\lambda D_{0}\times[0,\theta_{i-1}]}
. We can thus calculate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[H(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)^{\alpha}\eta\left((\lambda D_{0}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)))\times(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=2^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\eta\left((\lambda D_{0}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)))\times(\theta_{i-1},\theta_{i}]\right)\right|\eta_{|\lambda D_{0}\times[0,\theta_{i-1}]}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=2^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)^{\alpha}\big|\lambda D_{0}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x))\big|(\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1})\right].

(E.37)

Upper bounding the volume of the intersection by
\kappa_{d}R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)^{d}
and applying Lemma
E.8
yields that (
E.37
) is bounded by

2^{\alpha}\kappa_{d}(\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1})\mathbb{E}\left[R_{\theta_{i-1}}(x)^{\alpha+d}\right]\leqslant 2^{\alpha}\kappa_{d}(\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1})c\,\theta_{i-1}^{-\alpha/d-1},

(E.38)

with
c=c_{3}(\epsilon,d,\ell,\alpha+d)
. Combining this with (
E.34
), we infer that

\mathbb{E}\left[\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\right]\leqslant 2^{\alpha}\kappa_{d}c\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{i-1}^{-\alpha/d-1}(\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1})

(E.39)

for any partition
s=\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}<...<\theta_{n}=1
. Letting
n\rightarrow\infty
and the mesh of the partition tend to
0
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{\alpha}\kappa_{d}c\int_{s}^{1}\theta^{-\alpha/d-1}d\theta

\displaystyle=2^{\alpha}\kappa_{d}c\frac{d}{\alpha}\left(s^{-\alpha/d}-1\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant c^{\prime}s^{-\alpha/d},

(E.40)

with
c^{\prime}:=2^{\alpha}\kappa_{d}c_{3}(\epsilon,d,\ell,\alpha+d)\frac{d}{\alpha}
, a constant independent of
x
,
s
and
\lambda
.

Step 3.
To show the second part of (
E.30
), note that

\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\leqslant\opL_{(x,0)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}).

(E.41)

Indeed,
e(y,u,\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})
is independent of
(x,s)
and hence remains unchanged for any
(y,u)\in\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}
if we reduce the arrival time of
(x,s)
to zero. Any point connecting to
(x,s)
will also connect to
(x,0)
, hence no terms are deleted from the sum. Some positive terms might be added, since there may be points connecting to
(x,0)
but not to
(x,s)
.

For any
0<\theta_{0}<1
,

\opL_{(x,0)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})=H(0,\theta_{0})+\opL_{(x,\theta_{0})}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}).

(E.42)

A crude upper bound for the first term on the RHS is as follows:

H(0,\theta_{0})\leqslant\diam(\lambda D_{0})^{\alpha}\eta(B^{d}(x,\lambda\diam(D_{0}))\times[0,\theta_{0})).

(E.43)

Taking expectation, we get

\mathbb{E}H(0,\theta_{0})\leqslant\kappa_{d}\diam(D_{0})^{\alpha+d}\lambda^{\alpha+d}\theta_{0}.

(E.44)

On the other hand, by Step 2 we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\opL_{(x,\theta_{0})}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\right]\leqslant c^{\prime}\theta_{0}^{-\alpha/d}.

(E.45)

Combining (
E.41
)-(
E.45
) yields for any
0<\theta_{0}<1
that

\mathbb{E}\left[\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\right]\leqslant\kappa_{d}\diam(D_{0})^{\alpha+d}\lambda^{\alpha+d}\theta_{0}+c^{\prime}\theta_{0}^{-\alpha/d}.

(E.46)

Choosing
\theta_{0}:=\lambda^{-d}
, we deduce

\mathbb{E}\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}})\leqslant\left(\kappa_{d}\diam(D_{0})^{\alpha+d}+c^{\prime}\right)\lambda^{\alpha}.

(E.47)

The bound in (
E.30
) follows with
c_{4}(\epsilon,\ell,d,\alpha):=\kappa_{d}\diam(D_{0})^{\alpha+d}+c^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of Prop.
E.1
.

Proof of (
E.4
).
We will show that there is a constant
C_{1}>0
such that for all
(x,s)\in tH\times[0,1]
and
t\geqslant 1

\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s)}\big]\leqslant C_{1}R_{s}(x,tH,\eta_{tH})^{\alpha},

(E.48)

where
C_{1}
does not depend on
x,\ s
or
t
. The claim (
E.4
) then follows by Lemma
E.8
.

From here on, write
R_{s}(x)
for
R_{s}(x,tH,\eta_{tH})
.

By (
E.26
) and since
e(x,s,\eta_{tH})\leqslant R_{s}(x)
, which is measurable with respect to
\eta_{|tH\times[0,s)}
, it is enough to show that

\mathbb{E}\big[|\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s)}\big]\leqslant C_{2}R_{s}(x)^{\alpha},

(E.49)

for some constant
C_{2}>0
.

As established in the proof of Lemma
E.10
, all points connecting to
(x,s)
are points of
\eta
inside the set
A(t,x,s):=(tH\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{s}(x)))\times(s,1]
. Since this is included in
tH
, a set of finite measure, the total number of points in
\eta\cap A(t,x,s)
is almost surely finite. Denote the points connecting to
(x,s)
by
(y_{1},s_{1}),...,(y_{m},s_{m})
, ordered by increasing mark, i.e.
s_{1}<s_{2}<...<s_{m}
.

With this notation, we have

\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}e(y_{i},s_{i},\eta_{tH})^{\alpha}.

(E.50)

When removing all points of
\eta
outside of
A(t,x,s)
, i.e. all points further than
R_{s}(x)
from
x
and all points of mark less than
s
, then for each of the points
y_{2},...,y_{m}
, the edge-length to its online nearest neighbour in
\eta_{tH}
will either stay constant or increase. It will never be zero because
y_{1}
remains as a potential nearest neighbour. In formulas:

e(y_{i},s_{i},\eta_{tH})\leqslant e(y_{i},s_{i},\eta_{|A(t,x,s)}),\qquad\text{for }i=2,...,m.

(E.51)

For
i=1
, we know as in the proof of Lemma
E.10
that

e(y_{1},s_{i},\eta_{tH})\leqslant 2R_{s}(x).

(E.52)

Combining (
E.50
) with the above discussion, we get

\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}\leqslant 2^{\alpha}R_{s}(x)^{\alpha}+\sum_{i=2}^{m}e(y_{i},s_{i},\eta_{|A(t,x,s)}).

(E.53)

Using that edge-lengths are non-negative, we have

\opL_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}\leqslant 2^{\alpha}R_{s}(x)^{\alpha}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,u)\in\\
\eta\cap A(t,x,s)\end{subarray}}e(y,u,\eta_{|A(t,x,s)})^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(y,u)\rightarrow(x,s)\text{ in }\eta_{|A(t,x,s)}+\delta_{(x,s)}\}}.

(E.54)

Here we have added the edge contributions from points that would not connect to
(x,s)
in
\eta_{tH}+\delta_{(x,s)}
, but that do connect to
(x,s)
in the smaller set
A(t,x,s)+\delta_{(x,s)}
. The sum on the RHS on (
E.54
) still includes the points
(y_{i},s_{i})
for
i\geqslant 2
.

Observe that
R_{s}(x)>0
a.s. and define

D_{0}:=R_{s}(x)^{-1}\left(tH\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,R_{s}(x))\right).

(E.55)

Now we have
A(t,x,s)=R_{s}(x)D_{0}\times(s,1]
and we can write the second term in (
E.54
) as

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y,u)\in\\
\eta\cap(R_{s}(x)D_{0}\times(s,1])\end{subarray}}e(y,u,\eta_{|R_{s}(x)D_{0}\times(s,1]})^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(y,u)\rightarrow(x,s)\text{ in }\eta_{|R_{s}(x)D_{0}\times(s,1]}+\delta_{(x,s)}\}}.

(E.56)

By the properties of Poisson measures,
\eta_{|tH\times[0,s)}
(and hence also
R_{s}(x)
) is independent of
\eta_{|tH\times(s,1]}
. Conditioning on
\eta_{|tH\times[0,s)}
, the sum (
E.56
) is equal in law to
\opL_{(x,0)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{|\lambda D_{0}\times[0,1-s)}^{\prime})
, where
\lambda=R_{s}(x)
and
\eta^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
\eta
.

We clearly have that

\opL_{(x,0)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{|\lambda D_{0}\times[0,1-s)}^{\prime})\leqslant\opL_{(x,0)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{|\lambda D_{0}\times[0,1]}^{\prime})=\opL_{(x,0)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}^{\prime})

(E.57)

since adding points of higher mark only adds more edges to the graph.

By inequality (
E.30
) of Lemma
E.10
,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta^{\prime}}\left[\opL_{(x,0)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{\lambda D_{0}}^{\prime})\right]\leqslant c_{4}(\epsilon,\ell,d,\alpha)\lambda^{\alpha}=c_{4}(\epsilon,\ell,d,\alpha)R_{s}(x)^{\alpha},

(E.58)

where
\ell
is an upper bound for
\diam(D_{0})
and
\epsilon
is such that there is a point
x_{0}\in D_{0}
with
B^{d}(x_{0},\epsilon)\subset D_{0}
. Our claim (
E.49
) is proven if we can find such deterministic
\ell
and
\epsilon
that do not depend on
x,\ s
or
t
.

We have that

D_{0}=R_{s}(x)^{-1}tH\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(R_{s}(x)^{-1}x,1),

(E.59)

therefore
\diam(D_{0})\leqslant 2=:\ell
. To find an
\epsilon
, let
w:=t^{-1}x\in H
and
r:=t^{-1}R_{s}(x)
. Since
R_{s}(x)\leqslant t\diam(H)
, we have
r\leqslant\diam(H)
and by Lemma
E.3
, there exists a
0<q<1
depending on
H
and its properties such that there is a point
w^{\prime}\in H
with

B^{d}(w^{\prime},qr)\subset H\cap B^{d}(w,r).

(E.60)

Multiplying by
tR_{s}(x)^{-1}
yields

B^{d}(tR_{s}(x)^{-1}w^{\prime},q)\subset R_{s}(x)^{-1}\left(tH\cap B^{d}(x,R_{s}(x))\right)=D_{0}.

(E.61)

We can thus pick
\epsilon:=q
and the result is shown.

Proof of (
E.5
).
By Lemma
E.8
, it suffices to show that

\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,u)}\big]\leqslant cR_{u}(y)^{\alpha},

(E.62)

for some constant
c>0
.

By the triangle inequality,

|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\leqslant|\MalD_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{tH})|+|\MalD_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{tH}+\delta_{(x,s)})|.

(E.63)

The inequality (
E.48
) deals with the first term on the RHS. For the second term, we use (
E.26
) to say

|\MalD_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{tH}+\delta_{(x,s)})|\leqslant R_{u}(y,tH,\eta_{tH}+\delta_{(x,s)})^{\alpha}+\opL_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{tH}+\delta_{(x,s)}).

(E.64)

Reusing arguments from the proof of Lemma
E.10
, one sees that

R_{u}(y,tH,\eta_{tH}+\delta_{(x,s)})\leqslant R_{u}(y,tH,\eta_{tH}),

(E.65)

which yields the required bound for this term. On the other hand, if we remove a point of mark lower than the one of
y
, more points might connect to
y
, and no points already connected to
y
will change neighbour. Therefore

\opL_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{tH}+\delta_{(x,s)})\leqslant\opL_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\eta_{tH}).

(E.66)

The result now follows using (
E.49
).

Proof of (
E.6
).
Using ideas explained in
[
LPS16
, p.673]
, we will show that for any convex body
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for all
(x,s),(y,u)\in A\times[0,1]
with
s<u
and any finite set
\mu\subset A\times[0,1]
generic with respect to
(x,s)
and
(y,u)
, the condition

|x-y|>3R_{u}(y,A,\mu),

(E.67)

implies that
\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)=0
. This property is related to the theory of stabilisation, see
[
LPS16
, p.673]
for a discussion.
Recall that

\MalD_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)\\
=e(y,u,\mu)^{\alpha}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(z,v)\in\\
\mu\cap(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(u,1])\end{subarray}}\left(e(z,v,\mu+\delta_{(y,u)})^{\alpha}-e(z,v,\mu)^{\alpha}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{(z,v)\rightarrow(y,u)\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{(y,u)}\}}

(E.68)

and

\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu)=\MalD_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})-\MalD_{(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}(\mu).

(E.69)

Condition (
E.67
) implies that
A\not\subset\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(y,R_{u}(y,A,\mu))
and hence that
(y,u)
has an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
and
e(y,u,\mu)\neq 0
. Thus
e(y,u,\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})=|x-y|\wedge e(y,u,\mu)
, but at the same time

e(y,u,\mu)\leqslant R_{u}(y,A,\mu)<|x-y|.

(E.70)

It follows that
e(y,u,\mu+\delta_{(x,s)})=e(y,u,\mu)
. Let
(z,v)
be a point that connects to
(y,u)
in
\mu+\delta_{(y,u)}
. Then as shown previously,

|z-y|\leqslant R_{u}(y,A,\mu)\quad\text{and}\quad e(z,v,\mu)\leqslant 2R_{u}(y,A,\mu).

(E.71)

By conditions (
E.67
) and (
E.71
),

|x-z|\geqslant|x-y|-|z-y|>2R_{u}(y,A,\mu)\geqslant\max\{|z-y|,e(z,v,\mu)\}

(E.72)

and as before
e(z,v,\mu)\neq 0
, since
(y,u)
has an online nearest neighbour in
\mu
. Put together, this implies that
(z,v)
will not connect to
(x,s)
, neither in
\mu+\delta_{(x,s)}
, nor in
\mu+\delta_{(y,u)}+\delta_{(x,s)}
. It follows that
