Proof of Proposition
E.17
.

Combine Lemmas
E.19
-
E.25
.
∎

E.4
Proof of Theorem
5.3

The inequalities (
5.7
) and (
5.9
) are shown in Propositions
E.14
,
E.16
and
E.17
. In the following, we will write
c
to indicate the presence of a constant. The value of
c
might change from line to line, or indeed, within one line.

We have for
0<\alpha\leqslant\frac{d}{2}
:

|F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\leqslant(\diam(H)t)^{\alpha}\eta(tH\times[0,1])

(E.187)

and using (
E.26
),

|\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|\leqslant(\diam(H)t)^{\alpha}(\eta(tH\times[0,1])+1),

(E.188)

hence
F^{(\alpha)}_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})\cap\dom\MalD
.

E.4.1
Wasserstein distance when
\alpha=\frac{d}{2}

We use the bound given in Theorem
3.3
with
(\mathbb{Y},\bar{\lambda})=(tH\times[0,1],dx\otimes ds)
and
p=q=2
.

Recall that, combining Propositions
E.1
and
E.17
, we have for
r\geqslant 1
the following bounds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(d/2)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s)}\big]^{r}\right]^{1/r}\lesssim s^{-1/2}\wedge t^{d/2}

(E.189)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(d/2)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s\vee u)}\big]^{4}\right]^{1/4}\lesssim(s\vee u)^{-1/2}\exp(-c(s\vee u)|x-y|^{d}),

(E.190)

\displaystyle\var\left(F^{(d/2)}_{t}\right)\gtrsim t^{d}\log(t),

(E.191)

where for the second line we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to get

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(d/2)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s\vee u)}\big]^{4}\right]^{1/4}\\
\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(d/2)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s\vee u)}\big]^{8}\right]^{1/8}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(d/2)}_{t}|\big|\eta_{|tH\times[0,s\vee u)}\big]\neq 0)^{1/2}.

(E.192)

We start by plugging the bounds (
E.189
), (
E.190
), (
E.191
) into
\beta_{1}
from Theorem
3.3
. Then

\beta_{1}\lesssim\left(t^{d}\log(t)\right)^{-1}\left(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\left(\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ u^{-1/2}(u\vee s)^{-1/2}\exp\left(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d}\right)\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(E.193)

We now change variables as follows:

\begin{cases}\tilde{x}=t^{-1}x,\ dx=t^{d}d\tilde{x}\\
\tilde{y}=t^{-1}y,\ dy=t^{d}d\tilde{y}\\
\tilde{s}=t^{d}s,\ ds=t^{-d}d\tilde{s}\\
\tilde{u}=t^{d}u,\ du=t^{-d}d\tilde{u}\end{cases}

(E.194)

and deduce that
\beta_{1}
is bounded by

c\log(t)^{-1}\left(\int_{H}dx\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\left(\int_{H}dy\int_{0}^{\infty}du\ u^{-1/2}(u\vee s)^{-1/2}\exp(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d})\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(E.195)

This is
O(\log(t)^{-1})
since the integral is finite.
Indeed, changing variables in
y
and integrating over
x
, the integral is bounded by

|H|\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\left(\int_{B^{d}(0,2\diam(H))}dz\int_{0}^{\infty}du\ u^{-1/2}(u\vee s)^{-1/2}\exp(-c(u\vee s)|z|^{d})\right)^{2}.

(E.196)

Integrating over
z
, this is equal to

c\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}du\ u^{-1/2}(u\vee s)^{-3/2}(1-\exp(-c(u\vee s)))\right)^{2}.

(E.197)

Now we use that
1-\exp(-c(u\vee s))\lesssim 1\wedge(u\vee s)
to bound (
E.197
) by

c\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}du\ u^{-1/2}(u\vee s)^{-3/2}(1\wedge(u\vee s))\right)^{2}.

(E.198)

Splitting the integral over
s
and integrating over
u
, yields that (
E.198
) is equal to

c\int_{0}^{1}ds\ (3-\log(s))^{2}+c\int_{1}^{\infty}ds\ s^{-2}<\infty.

(E.199)

For
\beta_{2}
, we have

\beta_{2}\lesssim(t^{d}\log(t))^{-1}\left(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\left(\int_{tH}dy\int_{s}^{1}du\ u^{-1}\exp(-cu|x-y|^{d})\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(E.200)

This term can be dealt with in the same way as with
\beta_{1}
and it is
O(\log(t)^{-1})
.

As for
\beta_{3}
, we get

\beta_{3}\lesssim(t^{d}\log(t))^{-3/2}\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ (u^{-1/2}\wedge t^{d/2})^{3}.

(E.201)

Integrating over
u
and
y
gives that this is
O(\log(t)^{-3/2})
.

The last term is given by

\beta_{4}\lesssim(t^{d}\log(t))^{-3/2}\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{s}du\ u^{-1/2}s^{-1}\exp(-cs|x-y|^{d}).

(E.202)

Proceeding in the same way we dealt with
\beta_{1}
yields that
\beta_{4}=O(\log(t)^{-3/2})
.

Combining our estimates above, we conclude that
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}+\beta_{4}=O(\log(t)^{-1})
. ∎

E.4.2
Wasserstein and Kolmogorov distance when
\alpha<\frac{d}{2}

We use Theorem
3.4
with
p,q\in(1,2]
and
\epsilon>0
such that
2p(\alpha+\epsilon d)<d
and
(q+1)(\alpha+\epsilon d)<d
. In the following we will show that all terms
\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{7}
are
O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
. By Propositions
E.16
,
E.12
and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have for all
r\geqslant 1
the following bounds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\MalD_{(x,s)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|^{r}]^{1/r}\lesssim s^{-\alpha/d-\epsilon}

(E.203)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\MalD^{(2)}_{(x,s),(y,u)}F^{(\alpha)}_{t}|^{r}]^{1/r}\lesssim(u\vee s)^{-\alpha/d-\epsilon}\exp\left(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d}\right)

(E.204)

\displaystyle\var(F^{(\alpha)}_{t})\gtrsim t^{d}.

(E.205)

Introducing these bounds into
\gamma_{1}
, we get

\gamma_{1}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\left(\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ u^{-\alpha/d-\epsilon}(u\vee s)^{-\alpha/d-\epsilon}\exp\left(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d}\right)\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

(E.206)

Using a change of variables, we can bound the integral over
y
as follows:

\int_{tH}dy\ \exp(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d})\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dz\ \exp(-c(u\vee s)|z|^{d})\lesssim(u\vee s)^{-1}.

(E.207)

Introducing (
E.207
) into (
E.206
) and integrating over
x
gives us

\gamma_{1}\lesssim t^{d(1/p-1)}\left(\int_{0}^{1}ds\left(\int_{0}^{1}du\ u^{-\alpha/d-\epsilon}(u\vee s)^{-\alpha/d-\epsilon-1}\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

(E.208)

Integrating over
u
, one sees that these integrals are bounded by

c\int_{0}^{1}ds\ s^{-2p(\alpha/d+\epsilon)}<\infty.

(E.209)

Hence
\gamma_{1}=O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
.
The term
\gamma_{2}
is bounded by

\gamma_{2}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\left(\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ (u\vee s)^{-2\alpha/d-2\epsilon}\exp\left(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d}\right)\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

(E.210)

This can be treated analogously to the bound on
\gamma_{1}
and yields
\gamma_{2}=O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
.

As for
\gamma_{3}
, it is bounded by

\gamma_{3}\lesssim t^{-d(q+1)/2}\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ u^{-(q+1)(\alpha/d+\epsilon)}.

(E.211)

This is
O(t^{d(1-q)/2})
. Choose
q=3-\frac{2}{p}
, then
(q+1)(\alpha+\epsilon d)\leqslant 2p(\alpha+\epsilon d)<d
and
q\in(1,2]
, hence the conditions are satisfied. Moreover,
\frac{1-q}{2}=-1+\frac{1}{p}
, thus we find the same rate of convergence.

The term
\gamma_{4}
is bounded by

\gamma_{4}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\ s^{-2p(\alpha/d-\epsilon)}\right)^{1/p},

(E.212)

which is clearly
O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
.
For the term
\gamma_{5}
, we deduce

\gamma_{5}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ (u\vee s)^{-2p(\alpha/d+\epsilon)}\exp(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d})\right)^{1/p}.

(E.213)

Integrating over
x
and
y
as before, we infer

\gamma_{5}\lesssim t^{d(1/p-1)}\left(\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{0}^{1}du\ (u\vee s)^{-2p(\alpha/d+\epsilon)-1}\right)^{1/p},

(E.214)

which is
O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
.
The terms
\gamma_{6}
and
\gamma_{7}
work similarly:

\gamma_{6}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ (u\vee s)^{-p(\alpha/d+\epsilon)}s^{-p(\alpha/d+\epsilon)}\exp(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d})\right)^{1/p},

(E.215)

and

\gamma_{7}\lesssim t^{-d}\bigg(\int_{tH}dx\int_{0}^{1}ds\int_{tH}dy\int_{0}^{1}du\ (u\vee s)^{-(\alpha/d+\epsilon)}s^{-(\alpha/d+\epsilon)}\\
u^{-2(\alpha/d+\epsilon)(p-1)}\exp(-c(u\vee s)|x-y|^{d})\bigg)^{1/p}

(E.216)

which can be shown to be
O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
by the same method. This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.3
. ∎

Appendix F
Gilbert Graph

Throughout this section, we work in the framework of Section
5.2
and Theorem
5.5
. We start with a technical lemma.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
Z
be a Poisson random variable with intensity
\lambda>0
and let
r\geqslant 1
. Then there is a constant
c_{r}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}[Z^{r}]^{1/r}\leqslant c_{r}(\lambda\vee\lambda^{1/r}).

(F.1)

Proof.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. As can be found in any standard reference (see e.g.
[
PT11
, Proposition 3.3.2]
),

\mathbb{E}Z^{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}{m\brace i}\lambda^{i},

(F.2)

where
{m\brace i}
are the Stirling numbers of second kind. This is bounded by
(\lambda\vee\lambda^{m})B_{m}
, where
B_{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}{m\brace i}
is the
m
th Bell number. Hence

\mathbb{E}[Z^{m}]^{1/m}\leqslant B_{m}^{1/m}(\lambda\vee\lambda^{1/m}).

(F.3)

Now let
r>1
,
r\notin\mathbb{N}
and define
p_{0}:=\lfloor r\rfloor
and
p_{1}:=\lceil r\rceil
. Then take
\theta:=\frac{p_{1}}{r}\frac{r-p_{0}}{p_{1}-p_{0}}\in(0,1)
such that we have
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}
. By log-convexity of
L^{p}
norms (see e.g.
[
Tao
]
or
[
Bre11
, Remark 2. p.93]
) and the first part of the proof, we have

\mathbb{E}[Z^{r}]^{1/r}\leqslant\lVert Z\rVert_{p_{0}}^{1-\theta}\lVert Z\rVert_{p_{1}}^{\theta}\leqslant B_{p_{0}}^{(1-\theta)/p_{0}}B_{p_{1}}^{\theta/p_{1}}(\lambda\vee\lambda^{1/r}),

(F.4)

which provides the desired bound with
c_{r}:=B_{p_{0}}^{(1-\theta)/p_{0}}B_{p_{1}}^{\theta/p_{1}}
.
∎

As a next step, we prove bounds on the first and second order add-one costs of
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}
.

Proposition F.2
.

Let
\alpha>-\frac{d}{2}
and
r\geqslant 1
such that
d+r\alpha>0
. Then
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\in\dom\MalD
and there is a constant
c>0
such that for all
x,y\in W
and
t>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\right)^{r}\right]^{1/r}\leqslant c\epsilon_{t}^{\alpha}(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/r}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)^{1-1/r}

(F.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\neq 0)\leqslant 1\wedge\kappa_{d}t\epsilon_{t}^{d}

(F.6)

\displaystyle\MalD_{x,y}^{(2)}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<\epsilon_{t}\}}|x-y|^{\alpha}.

(F.7)

Proof.

As explained in Section
2
, to show that
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\in\dom\MalD
it suffices to argue that
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta^{t}})
and that (
2.3
) holds. By
[
RST17
, Theorem 3.1]
, it is true that
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{t}})
and the fact that
\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}
is square-integrable follows from (
F.5
) with
r=2
and from the fact that
W
is bounded. Hence
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\in\dom\MalD
follows once we have shown (
F.5
).

Since
\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}=L^{(\alpha)}_{t}(\eta^{t}+\delta_{x})-L^{(\alpha)}_{t}(\eta^{t})
, it is easy to see that

\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}=\sum_{y\in\eta^{t}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<\epsilon_{t}\}}|x-y|^{\alpha}.

(F.8)

It now follows that

\MalD_{x,y}^{(2)}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}=\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}(\eta^{t}+\delta_{y})-\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}(\eta^{t})=\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<\epsilon_{t}\}}|x-y|^{\alpha},

(F.9)

which gives (
F.7
). Since all terms in the sum (
F.8
) are non-negative, we have
\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\neq 0
if and only if
\eta^{t}(W\cap B^{d}(x,\epsilon_{t}))\neq 0
. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\neq 0)=\mathbb{P}(\eta^{t}(W\cap B^{d}(x,\epsilon_{t}))\neq 0)=1-\exp(-t|W\cap B^{d}(x,\epsilon_{t})|)

(F.10)

which is bounded by
1\wedge t|W\cap B^{d}(x,\epsilon_{t})|\leqslant 1\wedge\kappa_{d}t\epsilon_{t}^{d}
. This gives (
F.6
).

To prove (
F.5
), note first that
\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}
can be written

\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}=\int_{W\cap B^{d}(x,\epsilon_{t})}|x-y|^{\alpha}\eta^{t}(dy).

(F.11)

This is stochastically dominated by

\int_{B^{d}(x,\epsilon_{t})}|x-y|^{\alpha}\hat{\eta}^{t}(dy),

(F.12)

where
\hat{\eta}^{t}
is an
(\mathbb{R}^{d},t\,dx)
-Poisson measure. By translation invariance of the law of
\hat{\eta}^{t}
, this is equal in law to

\int_{B^{d}(0,\epsilon_{t})}|y|^{\alpha}\hat{\eta}^{t}(dy),

(F.13)

which in turn is equal in law to

\sum_{i=1}^{M_{t}}|U_{i}|^{\alpha},

(F.14)

where
M_{t}
is a Poisson random variable of intensity
\kappa_{d}t\epsilon_{t}^{d}
and
U_{1},U_{2},...
are i.i.d. uniform random variables in
B^{d}(0,\epsilon_{t})
independent of
M_{t}
.

By Jensen’s inequality,

\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{M_{t}}|U_{i}|^{\alpha}\right)^{r}\leqslant\mathbb{E}M_{t}^{r-1}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{t}}|U_{i}|^{r\alpha}.

(F.15)

By independence, this is equal to

\mathbb{E}\left[M_{t}^{r-1}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{t}}\mathbb{E}|U_{i}|^{r\alpha}\right]=\frac{d}{d+r\alpha}\epsilon_{t}^{r\alpha}\mathbb{E}M_{t}^{r}.

(F.16)

Using Lemma
F.1
, we deduce

\mathbb{E}\left[M_{t}^{r}\right]^{1/r}\leqslant c_{r}\left(\kappa_{d}t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\vee(\kappa_{d}t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/r}\right).

(F.17)

Combining the above bounds leads to

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\right)^{r}\right]^{1/r}\leqslant c_{r}(1\vee\kappa_{d})\left(\frac{d}{d+r\alpha}\right)^{1/r}\epsilon_{t}^{\alpha}\left(t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\vee(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/r}\right),

(F.18)

which shows (
F.5
).
∎

Theorem 3.3 in
[
RST17
]
gives us the following variance asymptotics: for
\alpha>-\frac{d}{2}
,

\var\left(L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\right)=\left(\sigma_{\alpha}^{(1)}t^{2}\epsilon_{t}^{2\alpha+d}+\sigma_{\alpha}^{(2)}t^{3}\epsilon_{t}^{2\alpha+2d}\right)|W|(1+O(\epsilon_{t})),

(F.19)

where
\sigma_{\alpha}^{(1)}=\frac{d\kappa_{d}}{2(d+2\alpha)}
and
\sigma_{\alpha}^{(2)}=\frac{d^{2}\kappa_{d}^{2}}{(\alpha+d)^{2}}
. Hence for large enough
t
, there is a constant
c>0
such that

\var\left(L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\right)\geqslant ct^{2}\epsilon_{t}^{2\alpha+d}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right).

(F.20)

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
5.5
.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

We plug the bounds from Proposition
F.2
and (
F.20
) into the terms
\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{7}
given in Theorem
3.4
. Let
p\in(1,2]
such that
2p\alpha+d>0
.

For the choice of
r=2p
in (
F.5
), the first term yields

\gamma_{1}\lesssim\left(t^{2}\epsilon_{t}^{2\alpha+d}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)\right)^{-1}\left(\int_{W}\left(\int_{W}\epsilon_{t}^{\alpha}(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/(2p)}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)^{1-1/(2p)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<\epsilon_{t}\}}|x-y|^{\alpha}tdy\right)^{p}tdx\right)^{1/p}.

(F.21)

Note that

\int_{W}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<\epsilon_{t}\}}|x-y|^{\alpha}dy\leqslant\int_{B^{d}(x,\epsilon_{t})}|x-y|^{\alpha}dy=\frac{d\kappa_{d}}{d+\alpha}\epsilon_{t}^{d+\alpha}.

(F.22)

Hence
\gamma_{1}
is (up to multiplication by a positive constant) bounded by

t^{1/p-1}(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/(2p)}(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{-1/(2p)}=t^{1/p-1}\left(1\wedge(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/(2p)}\right).

(F.23)

As for
\gamma_{2}
, we have

\gamma_{2}\lesssim\left(t^{2}\epsilon_{t}^{2\alpha+d}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)\right)^{-1}\left(\int_{W}\left(\int_{W}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<\epsilon_{t}\}}|x-y|^{2\alpha}tdy\right)^{p}tdx\right)^{1/p}.

(F.24)

By (
F.22
), we deduce

\gamma_{2}\lesssim t^{1/p-1}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)^{-1}.

(F.25)

For
\gamma_{3}
, take
q=3-\frac{2}{p}
. Then
q\in(1,2]
and
(q+1)\alpha+d>2p\alpha+d>0
. Let
r>\frac{q+1}{2}
such that
2r\alpha+d>0
. Then, using

\mathbb{E}\left(\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\right)^{q+1}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\right)^{2r}\right]^{\frac{q+1}{2r}}\mathbb{P}(\MalD_{x}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}\neq 0)^{1-\frac{q+1}{2r}}

(F.26)

we deduce

\gamma_{3}\lesssim\left(t^{2}\epsilon_{t}^{2\alpha+d}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)\right)^{1/p-2}\int_{W}\left(\epsilon_{t}^{\alpha}(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/(2r)}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)^{1-1/(2r)}\right)^{4-2/p}\left(1\wedge t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)^{1-\frac{4-2/p}{2r}}tdx.

(F.27)

Simplifying, we infer

\gamma_{3}\lesssim t^{1/p-1}(1\vee(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/p-1}).

(F.28)

With the same method, one can establish the upper bounds

\displaystyle\gamma_{4}\lesssim t^{1/p-1}\left(1\vee(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/p-1}\right)

(F.29)

\displaystyle\gamma_{5}\lesssim t^{1/p-1}(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/p-1}\left(1\vee t\epsilon_{t}^{d}\right)^{-1}

(F.30)

\displaystyle\gamma_{6}\lesssim t^{1/p-1}(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/p-1}\left(1\wedge(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/(2p)}\right)

(F.31)

\displaystyle\gamma_{7}\lesssim t^{1/p-1}(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{2/p-1-1/(2p^{2})}\left(1\vee(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})\right)^{-2/p+1+1/(2p^{2})}

(F.32)

All of these bounds are upper bounded by
t^{1/p-1}\left(1\vee(t\epsilon_{t}^{d})^{1/p-1}\right)
. If
\alpha>-\frac{d}{4}
, we can choose
p=2
and recover (
5.13
). To show (
5.14
), one chooses
1<p<-\frac{d}{2\alpha}
, thus concluding the proof.
∎

Appendix G
k
-Nearest Neighbour Graphs

In this section, we work in the setting of Theorem
5.7
. We start with a technical lemma.

Lemma G.1
.

Let
\phi:(0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,\infty)
be a non-increasing function satisfying (
5.16
) for some
r>2
. Then for any
0\leqslant q\leqslant r
, the following two integrals are finite:

\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)^{q}s^{d-1}ds<\infty

(G.1)

and for any constant
c>0
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(|x|)^{q}\exp(-c|x|^{d})dx<\infty.

(G.2)

Moreover, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
a finite generic set with respect to
x
, define the following quantity:

e(x,\mu):=\begin{cases}0&if $\mu=\emptyset$\\
\min\{|x-z|:z\in\mu,z\neq x\}&if $\mu\neq\emptyset$,\end{cases}

(G.3)

(that is,
e(x,\mu)
is the length from
x
to the point of
\mu
nearest to it, or zero if
\mu
is empty). Extend the definition of
\phi
to
[0,\infty)
by setting
\phi(0)=0
. Then for any
0<q\leqslant r
, there is a constant
c>0
such that for all
x\in tH
,
t\geqslant 1
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))^{q}\right]^{1/q}<c.

(G.4)

Proof.

We start by noting that
ds^{d-1}\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<s<1\}}
is a probability density. Hence, by Jensen’s inequality,

\left(d\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)^{q}s^{d-1}ds\right)^{1/q}\leqslant\left(d\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)^{r}s^{d-1}ds\right)^{1/r}.

(G.5)

This is finite by virtue of condition (
5.16
), thus yielding (
G.1
). Using polar coordinates, the integral in (
G.2
) is equal to

d\kappa_{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi(s)^{q}\exp(-cs^{d})s^{d-1}ds.

(G.6)

Now use that
\phi
is non-increasing to infer that, if
s\geqslant 1
, then
\phi(s)\leqslant\phi(1)
. Hence (
G.6
) is bounded by

d\kappa_{d}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)^{q}s^{d-1}ds+\int_{1}^{\infty}\phi(1)^{q}s^{d-1}\exp(-cs^{d})ds\right).

(G.7)

The second integral is clearly finite and the first is finite by (
G.1
), thus implying (
G.2
). For the bound (
G.4
), note that by Jensen’s inequality

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))^{r}\right]^{1/r},

(G.8)

therefore it suffices to show the bound for
q=r
.

Take
x\in tH
and let us study the distribution of
e(x,\eta_{|tH})
. For any
a\geqslant 0
, we have

G(a):=\mathbb{P}(e(x,\eta_{|tH})\leqslant a)=\mathbb{P}(e(x,\eta_{|tH})=0)+\mathbb{P}(0<e(x,\eta_{|tH})\leqslant a).

(G.9)

The event
e(x,\eta_{|tH})=0
happens if and only if
\eta(tH)=0
, whereas
0<e(x,\eta_{|tH})\leqslant a
is equivalent to the event that
\eta(tH\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,a))\neq 0
. Hence

G(a)=\mathbb{P}(\eta(tH)=0)+\mathbb{P}(\eta(tH\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{d}(x,a))\neq 0)=\exp(-|tH|)+1-\exp(-|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|).

(G.10)

We can compute the derivative of
1-\exp(-|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|)
as follows:

\frac{d}{da}(1-\exp(-|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|))=\exp(-|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|)\frac{d}{da}|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|.

(G.11)

Note that, by changing variables and moving to polar coordinates, we can rewrite the volume as

\displaystyle|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\in tH\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|y-x|<a\}}dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x+z\in tH\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|z|<a\}}dz

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{a}du\int_{S^{d-1}}d\omega\,u^{d-1}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x+u\omega\in tH\}},

(G.12)

where
S^{d-1}
is the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define thus

g(a):=\exp(-|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|)\int_{S^{d-1}}a^{d-1}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x+a\omega\in tH\}}d\omega,

(G.13)

then

G(a)=\mathbb{P}(e(x,\eta_{|tH})=0)+\int_{0}^{a}g(u)du.

(G.14)

Going back to the bound we want to prove, we now have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))^{r}\right]^{1/r}

\displaystyle=\left(\phi(0)\mathbb{P}(e(x,\eta_{|tH})=0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi(u)^{r}g(u)du\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi(u)^{r}g(u)du\right)^{1/r}.

(G.15)

We need to show that this integral is bounded by a constant independent of
t
. Since
H
has non-empty interior, there is a ball
B^{d}(x_{0},\delta)\subset H
, with
x_{0}\in H
and
\delta>0
. By Lemma
E.3
and rescaling, there is a constant
c_{H}>0
depending on
H
and
d
such that
|tH\cap B^{d}(x,a)|\geqslant c_{H}a^{d}
. Thus, upper bounding the indicator by
1
, we find

g(a)\leqslant d\kappa_{d}a^{d-1}\exp(-c_{H}a^{d}).

(G.16)

As a consequence

\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi(u)^{r}g(u)du\right)^{1/r}\leqslant\left(d\kappa_{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi(u)^{r}u^{d-1}\exp\left(-c_{H}u^{d}\right)du\right)^{1/r},

(G.17)

and the RHS of this inequality is finite by (
G.2
).
∎

Proposition G.2
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
5.7
, there are absolute constants
C_{2},c_{2}>0
such that for any
t\geqslant 1
, any
x,y\in tH
, the following bound holds:

\mathbb{P}\left(\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}\neq 0\right)<C_{2}\exp\left(-c_{2}|x-y|^{d}\right).

(G.18)

For any
1\leqslant p\leqslant\frac{r}{2}
there is a constant
c_{1}>0
such that for any
t\geqslant 1
, any
x,y\in tH
, the following two bounds hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{x}F_{t}|^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}<c_{1}

(G.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}<c_{1}\left(\phi(|x-y|)+1\right)

(G.20)

Moreover,
F_{t}\in\dom\MalD
.

The proof of (
G.18
) reuses arguments from
[
LPS16
, Theorem 7.1]
.

Proof.

Step 1.
We start by showing that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any finite set
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
generic with respect to
x
, we have
\MalD_{x}F(\mu)\geqslant 0
.

First, note that

F(\mu)=\sum_{e\in k\text{-NN}(\mu)}\phi(|e|),

(G.21)

where we take the sum over all edges
e
in the
k
-NN graph. Define a ‘direction’ on the graph in the following way:

•

If a point
w
is a nearest neighbour of the point
z
, direct the edge from
z
to
w
and write
z\rightarrow w
;

•

if
z
and
w
are reciprocal nearest neighbours, direct the edge both ways and write
z\leftrightarrow w
.

Upon addition of the point
x
, any of the following scenarios can happen:

1.

An edge
z\rightarrow w
is replaced by
z\rightarrow x
(or
z\leftrightarrow x
), in which case
|z-w|>|z-x|
;

2.

an edge
z\leftrightarrow w
is replaced by
z\rightarrow x
(or
z\leftrightarrow x
) and
w\rightarrow x
(or
w\leftrightarrow x
), in which case
|z-w|>|z-x|\vee|w-x|
;

3.

an edge
z\leftrightarrow w
becomes
w\rightarrow z
and the edge
z\rightarrow x
(or
z\leftrightarrow x
) is added;

4.

edges
x\rightarrow z
are added.

Let
E_{a}
and
E_{r}
be the sets of added and removed edges respectively. Every removed edge is replaced by at least one added edge with shorter length. All other added edges increase
\MalD_{x}F(\mu)
. Since
\phi
is decreasing, we have thus

\MalD_{x}F(\mu)=\sum_{e\in E_{a}}\phi(|e|)-\sum_{e\in E_{r}}\phi(|e|)\geqslant 0.

(G.22)

Step 2.
We now prove (
G.19
). Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a finite set generic with respect to
x
, and consider the
k
-NN built on
\mu
. Define
e(x,\mu)
as in Lemma
G.1
.

Suppose that
\mu\neq\emptyset
and consider
\MalD_{x}F(\mu)
. There is a constant
n_{d,k}
such that for any
k
-NN graph in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, no vertex has degree more than
n_{d,k}
. This fact was used in the proof of
[
LPS16
, Lemma 7.2]
and we refer to the references given therein for more details. When adding
x
to the graph, all added edges are incident to
x
, and hence we have
|E_{a}|\leqslant n_{d,k}
. Every added edge between points
x
and
y
must verify
|x-y|\geqslant e(x,\mu)
, since
e(x,\mu)
is the minimally possible edge-length for any edge incident to
x
. Since
\phi
is decreasing, we conclude

|\MalD_{x}F(\mu)|=\MalD_{x}F(\mu)\leqslant|E_{a}|\phi(e(x,\mu))\leqslant n_{d,k}\phi(e(x,\mu)).

(G.23)

Note that this is well-defined since
e(x,\mu)>0
if
\mu\neq\emptyset
.

If
\mu=\emptyset
, then
\MalD_{x}F(\mu)=0
. Extend the definition of
\phi
to
[0,\infty)
by setting
\phi(0)=0
. Then in all cases

|\MalD_{x}F(\mu)|\leqslant n_{d,k}\phi(e(x,\mu)).

(G.24)

We have thus the following bound:

\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{x}F_{t}|^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}\leqslant n_{d,k}\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)},

(G.25)

which is bounded by a constant by Lemma
G.1
.

Step 3.
We now show (
G.20
). By Step 2, we have

\displaystyle|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\MalD_{x}F(\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{x})|+|\MalD_{x}F(\eta_{|tH})|

\displaystyle\leqslant n_{d,k}\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))+n_{d,k}\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{y})).

(G.26)

For ease of notation, define the event
A_{t}:=\{\eta(tH)\neq 0\}
. If
\eta(tH)=0
, then
e(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{y})=|x-y|
, else
e(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{y})=e(x,\eta_{|tH})\wedge|x-y|
. Hence if we split over both events, we get

\displaystyle\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{y}))

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{A_{t}^{c}}\phi(|x-y|)+\mathbbm{1}_{A_{t}}\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH})\wedge|x-y|)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbbm{1}_{A_{t}^{c}}\phi(|x-y|)+\mathbbm{1}_{A_{t}}\phi(|x-y|)+\mathbbm{1}_{A_{t}}\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))

\displaystyle=\phi(|x-y|)+\mathbbm{1}_{A_{t}}\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH})).

(G.27)

Hence we have

|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|\leqslant 2n_{d,k}\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))+n_{d,k}\phi(|x-y|).

(G.28)

Lemma
G.1
now yields the result.

Step 4.
The inequality (
G.18
) follows immediately from the argument used in the proof of
[
LPS16
, Theorem 7.1]
, which relies solely on the structure of the graph, and not on the function applied to the edge-lengths.

Step 5.
Lastly, we show that
F_{t}\in\dom\MalD
. As was explained in Section
2
, it suffices to show that
F_{t}
is integrable and
\MalD_{x}F_{t}
square integrable. The second fact immediately follows from (
5.16
). By Mecke equation we also have

\mathbb{E}F_{t}=\int_{tH}\int_{tH}\phi(|x-y|)\mathbb{P}(x\in N(y,\eta_{|tH})\text{ or }y\in N(x,\eta_{|tH}))dxdy,

(G.29)

where we recall that
N(y,\eta_{|tH})
is the set of the
k
nearest neighbours of
y
in
\eta_{|tH}
.
Upper bounding the probability in the integrand by
1
and changing variables, this is upper bounded by

t^{d}|H|\int_{B^{d}(0,t\diam(H))}\phi(|z|)dz.

(G.30)

Changing to polar coordinates, this is equal to

t^{d}|H|d\kappa_{d}\int_{0}^{t\diam(H)}s^{d-1}\phi(s)ds,

(G.31)

which is finite by Lemma
G.1
.
∎

Proposition G.3
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
5.7
, there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

c_{1}t^{d}\leqslant\var(F_{t})\leqslant c_{2}t^{d}.

(G.32)

Proof.

The upper bound immediately follows from Poincaré inequality ((
4.7
) with
p=2
) and (
G.19
) with
p=1
. For the lower bound, we use
[
LPS16
, Theorem 5.2]
and a reasoning similar to what was done in the proof of
[
LPS16
, Lemma 7.2]
.

Assume there are points
w_{1},...,w_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\frac{1}{2}<|w_{i}|<1
for
i\in\{1,...,m\}
such that for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|y|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
,

|\{i\in\{1,...,m\}:|w_{i}-y|<|y|\}|\geqslant k+1.

(G.33)

This means that for any point
y
outside
B^{d}\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
, there are at least
k+1
points among the
\{w_{1},...,w_{m}\}
which are closer to
y
that the origin.

Now assume that there is
\tau>0
and
x\in tH
such that
B^{d}(x,\tau)\in tH
. Define
\tilde{w}_{i}:=x+\tau w_{i}
. Consider the collection of points

\mathcal{U}:=\eta_{|tH}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\tilde{w}_{i}}

(G.34)

and let us evaluate
\MalD_{x}F(\mathcal{U})
.

First, note that by (
G.33
), all points outside
B^{d}(x,\tau/2)
have at least
k
points closer than
x
among the
\tilde{w}_{1},...,\tilde{w}_{m}
, including these points themselves. Therefore any points in
\mathcal{U}
connecting to
x
must be within
B^{d}(x,\tau/2)
.

Assume
\eta(B^{d}(x,\tau))=0
. Then the only edges added when adding
x
are those from
x
to its
k
nearest neighbours among
\tilde{w}_{1},...,\tilde{w}_{m}
. Since
|x-\tilde{w}_{i}|\leqslant\tau
for any
i
, we have:

\mathbbm{1}_{\{\eta(B^{d}(x,\tau))=0\}}\MalD_{x}F(\mathcal{U})\geqslant\mathbbm{1}_{\{\eta(B^{d}(x,\tau))=0\}}k\phi(\tau).

(G.35)

As shown in Step 1 of the proof of Proposition
G.2
, we have
\MalD_{x}F(\mathcal{U})\geqslant 0
and

\left|\mathbb{E}\MalD_{x}F(\mathcal{U})\right|\geqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\eta(B^{d}(x,\tau))=0\}}k\phi(\tau)\right]=k\phi(\tau)\exp(-\kappa_{d}\tau^{d}).

(G.36)

We now find a set of
(x,\tilde{w}_{1},...,\tilde{w}_{m})
for which this bound is true.

Let
\tau>0
be such that there is a ball
B^{d}(x_{0},2\tau)\subset H
. For any
x\in B^{d}(tx_{0},t\tau)
we have
B^{d}(x,\tau)\subset tH
.

The closure of the set
B^{d}(0,1)\setminus B^{d}\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
is compact and can be covered by balls of radius
\frac{1}{4}
. Setting
k+1
points into the interior of each intersection of one such ball with the annulus
B^{d}(0,1)\setminus B^{d}\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
gives a collection of points
\{z_{1},...,z_{m}\}
. We claim that this collection satisfies (
G.33
). Indeed, any point inside
B^{d}(0,1)\setminus B^{d}\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
will be in one ball of the covering and thus there are at least
k+1
points from
\{z_{1},...,z_{m}\}
at a distance of at most
\frac{1}{2}
. For any point
y
outside
B^{d}(0,1)
, there is a point
z\in\partial B^{d}(0,1)
such that
|y-z|=|y|-1
and this point has
k+1
points
z_{i}
among
\{z_{1},...,z_{m}\}
that are less than
\frac{1}{2}
away. For any such
z_{i}
, one has
|y-z_{i}|\leqslant|y|-1+\frac{1}{2}
.

Given a choice of
z_{1},...,z_{m}
, property (
G.33
) still holds if we slightly perturb the
z_{i}
: there is an
\epsilon>0
such that the collection of points
\{z_{1}+y_{1},...,z_{m}+y_{m}\}
satisfies (
G.33
) for any
y_{1},...,y_{m}\in B^{d}(0,\epsilon)
.

The bound (
G.36
) is true for any
(x,\tilde{w}_{1},...,\tilde{w}_{m})\in U
, where

U:=\left\{(z,z+\tau(z_{1}+y_{1}),...,z+\tau(z_{m}+y_{m})):z\in B^{d}(tx_{0},t\tau),y_{1},...,y_{m}\in B^{d}(0,\epsilon)\right\}.

(G.37)

By
[
LPS16
, Theorem 5.2]
and a development analogous to the one in the proof of
[
LPS16
, Theorem 5.3]
, we find that for some constant
c>0
,

\var(F_{t})\geqslant c|U|=c\kappa_{d}\tau^{d}(\kappa_{d}\epsilon^{d})^{m}t^{d},

(G.38)

which yields the desired bound.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.7
.

For the rest of the proof, all constants will be referred to as
c
, to simplify notation. Take
p\in(1,2]
such that
p<\frac{r}{2}
, where
r
is given by the condition (
5.16
).

Let us start with a bound on
\gamma_{1}
. We use that

\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}\neq 0\right)^{1/(2p)-1/r}\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|^{r}\right]^{1/r}

(G.39)

and the bounds in
G.2
and
G.3
to conclude that

\gamma_{1}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}\left(\int_{tH}\exp(-c|x-y|^{d})(\phi(|x-y|)+1)dy\right)^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}.

(G.40)

After changing variables and extending the domain of integration to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the inner integral is upper bounded by

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp(-c|y|^{d})(\phi(|y|)+1)dy,

(G.41)

which is finite by Lemma
G.1
. We deduce that

\gamma_{1}\lesssim t^{d(1/p-1)}.

(G.42)

The terms
\gamma_{2},\gamma_{4},\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6},\gamma_{7}
can be shown to be
O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
by applying the same strategy.

For
\gamma_{3}
, take
q=3-\frac{2}{p}
. Then
q+1<2p<r
and
\mathbb{E}|\MalD_{y}F_{t}|^{q+1}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{y}F_{t}|^{r}\right]^{(q+1)/r}
and we have

\gamma_{3}\lesssim t^{-d(2-1/p)}\int_{tH}\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{y}F_{t}|^{r}\right]^{(q+1)/r}dx\lesssim t^{d(1/p-1)}.

(G.43)

To show that in particular this bound is true for the function
\phi(x)=x^{-\alpha}
with
0<\alpha<\frac{d}{2}
, it suffices to check condition (
5.16
). Indeed, let
2<r<\frac{d}{\alpha}
. Then

\int_{0}^{1}s^{-\alpha r}s^{d-1}ds<\infty

(G.44)

and the bound on the speed of convergence holds for any
p<\frac{r}{2}<\frac{d}{2\alpha}
, with
p\in(1,2]
.
∎

Appendix H
Radial Spanning Tree

In this section, we work in the setting of Theorem
5.8
and we call finite sets
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
generic only if
\mu
is generic with respect to
0
.

Proposition H.1
.

There are absolute constants
c_{1},C_{1}>0
(independent of
\phi
) such that for any
t\geqslant 1
and any
x,y\in tH
, the following bound holds:

\mathbb{P}\left(\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}\neq 0\right)\leqslant C_{1}\exp(-c_{1}|x-y|^{d}).

(H.1)

Moreover, for any
1\leqslant p\leqslant\frac{r}{2}
, there are constants
c_{2},C_{2}>0
such that for any
t\geqslant 1
and any
x,y\in tH
, the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{x}F_{t}|^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}\leqslant C_{2}\left(1+\phi(|x|)\exp(-c_{2}|x|^{d})\right)

(H.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}\leqslant C_{2}\left(\phi(|x-y|)\exp(-c_{2}|x-y|^{d})+1\right)

(H.3)

We also have that
F_{t}\in\dom\MalD
.

The proof relies on and reuses some arguments from the proofs of Lemmas 4.1 and 4.2 in
[
ST17
]
.

Proof.

The bound (
H.1
) follows immediately from the proof of Lemma 4.2 in
[
ST17
]
. Indeed, the proof given in
[
ST17
]
does not depend on the chosen functional, but only on the structure of the graph.

Next, we establish that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
finite and generic with respect to
x
, we have
\MalD_{x}F(\mu)\geqslant 0
. Indeed, it is true that

\MalD_{x}F(\mu)=\phi(g(x,\mu))+\sum_{y\in\mu}\left(\phi(g(y,\mu+\delta_{x}))-\phi(g(y,\mu))\right).

(H.4)

Since
0<g(y,\mu+\delta_{x})\leqslant g(y,\mu)
and
\phi
is non-increasing, the above expression is non-negative.

For the next part, define
e(x,\mu)
as we did in Lemma
G.1
and extend
\phi
by setting
\phi(0)=0
. Then note that the summand on the RHS of (
H.4
) is zero, unless
y
connects to
x
, in which case
g(y,\mu+\delta_{x})=|x-y|
. It follows that

0\leqslant\MalD_{x}F(\mu)\leqslant\phi(g(x,\mu))+\sum_{y\in\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}\phi(|x-y|).

(H.5)

If
\mu
is non-empty, then
|x-y|\geqslant e(x,\mu)
, hence the second term on the RHS of (
H.4
) is upper bounded by

\phi(e(x,\mu))\sum_{y\in\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}},

(H.6)

a bound that continues to hold if
\mu=\emptyset
.

As for the first term on the RHS of (
H.4
), if
\mu\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|)
is empty, then the term is equal to
\phi(|x|)
. If not, then
g(x,\mu)\geqslant e(x,\mu)
and it is upper bounded by
\phi(e(x,\mu))
. Hence we deduce the bound

\MalD_{x}F(\mu)\leqslant\phi(|x|)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mu\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|)\neq\emptyset\}}+\phi(e(x,\mu))\left(1+\sum_{y\in\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}\right).

(H.7)

It follows by Hölder’s and Minkowski’s inequalities that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\MalD_{x}F(\mu)\right)^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}\leqslant\phi(|x|)\mathbb{P}(\eta(tH\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|))=0)^{1/(2p)}\\
+\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))^{r}\right]^{1/r}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(1+\sum_{y\in\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}\bigg)^{m}\bigg]^{1/m},

(H.8)

where
m=\lceil\frac{2pr}{r-2p}\rceil
.

We start with the first term on the RHS of (
H.8
). We know that

\mathbb{P}(\eta(tH\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|))=0)=\exp(-|tH\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|)|).

(H.9)

Note that
B^{d}(\frac{x}{2},\frac{|x|}{2})\subset B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|)
, therefore

\left|tH\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|)\right|\geqslant\left|tH\cap B^{d}\left(\frac{x}{2},\frac{|x|}{2}\right)\right|.

(H.10)

Since
H
is non-empty, there is a ball
B^{d}(x_{0},\delta)\subset H
with
x_{0}\in H
and
\delta>0
. By scaling and use of Lemma
E.3
, we conclude that there is a constant
c_{H}>0
depending on
H
and
d
such that

|tH\cap B^{d}\left(\tfrac{x}{2},\tfrac{|x|}{2}\right)|\geqslant c_{H}|x|^{d}.

(H.11)

Hence

\phi(|x|)\mathbb{P}\left(\eta(tH\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|))=0\right)^{1/(2p)}\leqslant\phi(|x|)\exp\left(-\tfrac{c_{H}}{2p}|x|^{d}\right).

(H.12)

For the second term on the RHS of (
H.8
), we use that
\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(e(x,\eta_{|tH}))^{r}\right]^{1/r}
is bounded by a constant independent of
t
and
x
, by Lemma
G.1
. For the other part of the second term in (
H.8
), one can easily adapt the argument in the proof of
[
ST17
, Lemma 4.1]
to show that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(1+\sum_{y\in\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}\right)^{m}\right]^{1/m}

(H.13)

is uniformly bounded by a constant. This concludes the proof of (
H.2
).

Now we prove (
H.3
). For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
finite generic with respect to
x,y
, we have

\displaystyle\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F(\mu)

\displaystyle=\phi(g(x,\mu+\delta_{y}))+\sum_{z\in\mu}\left(\phi(|x-z|)-\phi(g(z,\mu+\delta_{y}))\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{y}+\delta_{x}\}}

\displaystyle+\left(\phi(|x-y|)-\phi(g(y,\mu))\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{y}+\delta_{x}\}}

\displaystyle-\phi(g(x,\mu))-\sum_{z\in\mu}\left(\phi(|x-z|)-\phi(g(z,\mu))\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}.

(H.14)

This expression is in fact symmetric in
x
and
y
since the operator
\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}
is symmetric. Assume without loss of generality that
|x|\leqslant|y|
. Then
x
cannot connect to
y
and hence
\phi(g(x,\mu+\delta_{y}))=\phi(g(x,\mu))
. The point
y
will connect to
x
if and only if
|x-y|<|y|
and
\mu\cap B^{d}(0,|y|)\cap B^{d}(y,|x-y|)=\emptyset
, thus

\left(\phi(|x-y|)-\phi(g(y,\mu))\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{y\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{y}+\delta_{x}\}}\\
=\phi(|x-y|)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<|y|\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mu\cap B^{d}(0,|y|)\cap B^{d}(y,|x-y|)=\emptyset\}}.

(H.15)

Moreover, a point
z
that connects to
x
in
\mu+\delta_{y}+\delta_{x}
also connects to
x
in
\mu+\delta_{x}
and in this case
\phi(|x-z|)\geqslant\phi(g(z,\mu+\delta_{y}))
. We deduce that

\sum_{z\in\mu}\left(\phi(|x-z|)-\phi(g(z,\mu+\delta_{y}))\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{y}+\delta_{x}\}}\leqslant\sum_{z\in\mu}\phi(|x-z|)\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}.

(H.16)

Lastly, if
z
connects to
x
in
\mu+\delta_{x}
, then
\phi(|x-z|)\geqslant\phi(g(z,\mu))
, thus

\sum_{z\in\mu}\left(\phi(|x-z|)-\phi(g(z,\mu))\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}\leqslant\sum_{z\in\mu}\phi(|x-z|)\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\rightarrow x\text{ in }\mu+\delta_{x}\}}.

(H.17)

Combining (
H.15
), (
H.16
) and (
H.17
), we deduce from (
H.14
) that

\left|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}\right|\leqslant\phi(|x-y|)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x-y|<|y|\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\eta(tH\cap B^{d}(0,|y|)\cap B^{d}(y,|x-y|))=0\}}+2\sum_{z\in\eta_{|tH}}\phi(|x-z|)\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\rightarrow x\text{ in }\eta\}}.

(H.18)

The proof of (
H.2
) shows that the
2p
th moment of the second term is uniformly bounded. When
|x-y|<|y|
, then
B^{d}\left(y(1-\frac{|x-y|}{2|y|}),\frac{|x-y|}{2}\right)
is included in the intersection
B^{d}(0,|y|)\cap B^{d}(y,|x-y|)
. By Lemma
E.3
, and rescaling, we have thus for some constant
c_{H}>0
that

\mathbb{P}(\eta(tH\cap B^{d}(0,|y|)\cap B^{d}(y,|x-y|))=0)\\
=\exp(-|tH\cap B^{d}(0,|y|)\cap B^{d}(y,|x-y|)|)\leqslant\exp(-c_{H}|x-y|^{d}).

(H.19)

The bound on the
2p
th moment of the first term in (
H.18
) follows.

The fact that
\MalD_{x}F_{t}
is square-integrable follows from the bound (
H.2
) and Lemma
G.1
. To show that
F_{t}\in\dom\MalD
, it suffices to show that
F_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\eta})
, as was explained in Section
2
. We apply Mecke equation and the fact that
g(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{x})=g(x,\eta_{|tH})
to deduce that

\mathbb{E}F_{t}=\int_{tH}\mathbb{E}\phi(g(x,\eta_{|tH}))dx.

(H.20)

As shown in the discussion leading to (
H.7
), this is bounded by

\int_{tH}\left(\phi(|x|)\mathbb{P}\left(\eta(tH\cap B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|))=0\right)+\phi(e(x,\mu))\right)dx.

(H.21)

By (
H.12
) and Lemma
G.1
, this integral is finite.
∎

Proposition H.2
.

Under the conditions above, there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for all
t\geqslant 1
large enough

c_{1}t^{d}\leqslant\var F_{t}\leqslant c_{2}t^{d}.

(H.22)

Proof.

The upper bound follows from (
H.2
) with
p=1
and similar integration arguments as in the proof of Proposition
H.1
. For the lower bound, we use Theorems 5.2 and 5.3 in
[
LPS16
]
again.

Recall that
B^{d}(0,\epsilon)\subset H
and let
\delta<\frac{\epsilon}{4}
. Fix
t\geqslant 1
and define the set

U:=\{(x,z):x\in tH\setminus B^{d}(0,4\delta),z\in B^{d}((1-\tfrac{\delta}{|x|})x,\delta)\}.

(H.23)

Note that
B^{d}\left(\left(1-\frac{\delta}{|x|}\right)x,\delta\right)\subset B^{d}(0,|x|)\cap B^{d}(x,|x|)
, therefore any
z\in B^{d}((1-\frac{\delta}{|x|})x,\delta)
verifies
|x-z|<|x|
. Moreover,
|x-z|\leqslant\left|x-z-\frac{\delta}{|x|}x\right|+\left|\frac{\delta}{|x|}x\right|\leqslant 2\delta
.

Now for
(x,z)\in U
, consider
\MalD_{x}F(\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{z})
. As can be seen from (
H.4
), we have

\MalD_{x}F(\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{z})\geqslant\phi(g(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{z})).

(H.24)

By our choice of
z
, we have
g(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{z})\leqslant|x-z|\leqslant 2\delta
. As
\phi
is decreasing, it follows that
\phi(g(x,\eta_{|tH}+\delta_{z}))\geqslant\phi(2\delta)
. By
[
LPS16
, Theorem 5.2]
,

\var(F_{t})\geqslant\frac{\phi(2\delta)^{2}}{4^{3}\cdot 2}\min_{\emptyset\neq J\subset\{1,2\}}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\subset U\\
\lambda(V)\geqslant\lambda(U)/8\end{subarray}}\lambda^{(|J|)}(\Pi_{J}(V)),

(H.25)

where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure.

By the reasoning in the proof of
[
LPS16
, Theorem 5.3]
, this quantity is lower bounded by
\lambda(U)
up to multiplication by a constant. We have

\lambda(U)=\kappa_{d}\delta^{d}|tH\setminus B^{d}(0,4\delta)|=\kappa_{d}\delta^{d}(t^{d}|H|-\kappa_{d}(4\delta)^{d}),

(H.26)

which is of order
t^{d}
for
t
large enough.
∎

In the development below, most constants will be called
c
for convenience. These constants are by no means the same and can (and will) change from line to line or indeed, within lines.

Proof of Theorem
5.8
.

We apply the bounds found in Propositions
H.1
and
H.2
to the terms
\gamma_{1}
,…,
\gamma_{7}
in Theorem
3.4
. Let
p\in(1,2]
such that
2p<r
and choose
s>0
such that
2p<s<r
. Then by Hölder’s inequality and (
H.3
) and (
H.1
),

\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|^{2p}\right]^{1/(2p)}\lesssim\exp(-c|x-y|^{d})\mathbb{E}\big[|\MalD^{(2)}_{x,y}F_{t}|^{s}\big]^{1/s}\lesssim\exp(-c|x-y|^{d})(1+\phi(|x-y|)).

(H.27)

With this bound, we find that
\gamma_{1}
is bounded by

\gamma_{1}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}\left(\int_{tH}\left(1+\phi(|x|)\exp(-c|x|^{d})\right)\exp(-c|x-y|^{d})\left(1+\phi(|x-y|)\right)dy\right)^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}.

(H.28)

Using Lemma
G.1
, we find
\gamma_{1}=O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
. The same way, we have
\gamma_{2}=O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
. For
\gamma_{3}
, take
q=3-\frac{2}{p}
, then
q+1<2p<s
and
\mathbb{E}|\MalD_{y}F_{t}|^{q+1}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[|\MalD_{y}F_{t}|^{s}\right]^{(q+1)/s}
. It follows that

\gamma_{3}\lesssim t^{-d(2-1/p)}\int_{tH}(1+\phi(|x|)\exp(-c|x|^{d}))^{q+1}dx,

(H.29)

which is of order
O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
. The terms
\gamma_{4},\gamma_{5}
and
\gamma_{6}
work much the same. For
\gamma_{7}
we use the simplified version proposed in Remark
3.5
and get

\gamma_{7}\lesssim t^{-d}\left(\int_{tH}\int_{tH}\exp(-c|x-y|^{d})(\phi(|x-y|)+1)(1+\phi(|x|)\exp(-c|x|^{d}))^{2p-1}dxdy\right)^{1/p}.

(H.30)

By the same methods as applied before, this is
O(t^{d(1/p-1)})
.

The claim that the bound (
5.21
) is true in particular for
\phi(s)=s^{-\alpha}
with
0<\alpha<\frac{d}{2}
follows from the discussion at the end of the proof of Theorem
5.7
.
∎
